Browse All / Ganglioneuroma

Ganglioneuroma

MONDO:0005033 Pathograph 13 Show in embeddings browser neuroblastic tumor

Ganglioneuroma is a benign peripheral neuroblastic tumor of the sympathetic nervous system and the most mature form in the family that also includes neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroblastoma. Its ganglion cells lie singly or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma, whose cells incorporate the neurites of the ganglion cells, so that no naked neurites are identified around them. The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (INPC) makes ganglioneuroma its Schwannian stroma-dominant category, with a maturing subtype, which besides fully mature ganglion cells contains a minor component of scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, and a mature subtype, whose ganglion cells are all completely mature; the INPC always classifies ganglioneuroma as favorable histology. Ganglioneuroma is usually diagnosed in older children and adults. In German trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 144 of 162 ganglioneuromas (88.9%) were of the maturing subtype, and median age at diagnosis was 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature ganglioneuroma, against 9 months for neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma taken together. In the cited hospital series the tumors arose most often in the retroperitoneum or elsewhere in the abdomen, including the adrenal gland, and next in the thorax, mainly in the posterior mediastinum; fewer arose in the presacral space or the neck, and many were found incidentally. Pain was the commonest symptom leading to diagnosis in the German trials, and watery diarrhea that the authors attributed to tumor secretion of vasoactive intestinal peptide has been described in case reports. In two immunohistochemistry series every ganglioneuroma tested expressed PHOX2B, and in one hospital's tumors phosphorylated AKT, mTOR and S6 were detected more often in ganglioneuroma than in poorly differentiated neuroblastoma. No initiating lesion has been described; MYCN amplification has been found in a few maturing ganglioneuromas. In the German trials urinary catecholamine metabolites were raised, and MIBG was taken up, less often in ganglioneuroma than in intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or in neuroblastoma. Treatment is surgical resection or, for selected asymptomatic tumors, observation. In the German trials no tumor progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, chemotherapy had no substantial effect in the 11 patients who received it, 2 of them with ganglioneuroma, no patient died of tumor progression, and the one patient with ganglioneuroma who died had complications of surgery. The cited surgical series report postoperative complication rates of 19% to 43.1%, counted per patient or per operation and in two series pooled with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and one patient in a 51-patient series of resected retroperitoneal tumors died after surgery. Whether every ganglioneuroma arises by maturation of a neuroblastoma is unproven (discussion gn_origin).

Subtypes

2
Ganglioneuroma, maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant) NCIT:C42064
VERY_FREQUENT
In the INPC, maturing ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant) is composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells; the fully mature ganglion cells are always covered with satellite cells. The INPC assigns tumors in this category to its favorable histology group. The subtype corresponds to the tumor group that the earlier Shimada classification called ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated. In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 144 of 162 ganglioneuromas (88.9%) were maturing, with a median age at diagnosis of 71 months; the very-frequent band describes that cohort. In CCG/COG studies, all 36 patients with maturing ganglioneuroma and a known stage had INSS stage 1-3 disease.
Show evidence (7 references)
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"Ganglioneuroma-Maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant): Composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells. Fully..."
Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases restates the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification definition of the ganglioneuroma-maturing subtype (Schwannian stroma-dominant). The tumor is mostly ganglioneuromatous stroma, with a minor component of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells beside fully mature ganglion cells. The fully mature ganglion cells are always covered with satellite cells, and tumors in this category are classified as favorable histology. It is a classification rule restated from the INPC papers, not a result of this cohort.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"According to the INPC, tumors in the GN category are further classified into GN-Maturing subtype (the same tumor group described as “Ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated” in the Shimada classification system3) and GN-Mature subtype (completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor)."
In the introduction of a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group (CCG/COG) clinicopathological series, Okamatsu et al. restate the INPC subdivision of the ganglioneuroma category: GN-Maturing, which they equate with the tumor group that the earlier Shimada classification (the system the INPC adapted) called ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated, and GN-Mature, the completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor. This is the split the Maturing and Mature subtypes encode. The sentence restates a published classification; it is not a result from the paper's own cohort.
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee, whose six member pathologists defined the morphologic criteria from a review of 227 cases, classifies neuroblastic tumors into four categories. Ganglioneuroma is the third, the Schwannian stroma-dominant category, with maturing and mature subtypes. The abstract prints the composite label of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma as 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant', and the quote keeps that spelling. The abstract does not assign ganglioneuroma to a prognostic group; the classification itself is reported in the companion article (PMID:10421273).
+ 4 more references
Ganglioneuroma, mature (Schwannian stroma-dominant) NCIT:C42065
OCCASIONAL
The completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor: ganglioneuroma whose ganglion cells are all completely mature, in Schwannian stroma. Completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, in either subtype. In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 18 of 162 ganglioneuromas (11.1%) were mature, with a median age at diagnosis of 125 months; the occasional band describes that cohort, which left out older patients. Mature ganglioneuroma was not eligible for the CCG/COG studies and was excluded from the CCG/COG series of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma, whose ganglioneuroma data therefore cover the maturing subtype only.
Show evidence (7 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"They are either completely mature (mature GN) or a mixture of mature and maturing ganglion cells (maturing GN). Completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells."
In the 2026 review's paragraph on the ganglioneuroma category these sentences separate the two INPC subtypes: in mature GN the ganglion cells are completely mature, while maturing GN mixes mature and maturing ganglion cells. The review adds that completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, which holds for such cells in either subtype. This restates the INPC subtype criteria rather than reporting case data.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"According to the INPC, tumors in the GN category are further classified into GN-Maturing subtype (the same tumor group described as “Ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated” in the Shimada classification system3) and GN-Mature subtype (completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor)."
In the introduction of a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group (CCG/COG) clinicopathological series, Okamatsu et al. restate the INPC subdivision of the ganglioneuroma category into GN-Maturing and GN-Mature, the completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor. The sentence restates a published classification; it is not a result from the paper's own cohort.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT Human Clinical
"Cases with the diagnosis of GN-Mature subtype were excluded from the analysis, since they were not eligible for the CCG/COG studies."
Okamatsu et al. state that cases diagnosed as the mature subtype of ganglioneuroma (GN-Mature) were not eligible for the Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group (CCG/COG) studies and were excluded from their analysis. This CCG/COG series therefore holds no mature ganglioneuroma, and its ganglioneuroma cases are the maturing subtype only. The sentence states ineligibility; it gives no reason for it and does not say that no case with this diagnosis was ever registered on a CCG/COG study.

Discussions and Knowledge Gaps

1
Does every ganglioneuroma arise by maturation of a neuroblastoma or ganglioneuroblastoma, or can some arise without passing through a neuroblastoma or ganglioneuroblastoma stage?
CONTROVERSY OPEN gn_origin
Attached to
pathophysiology#Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation pathophysiology#AKT-mTOR-S6 Pathway Activation
Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means ganglioneuroma arises by maturation of a neuroblastic tumor; REFUTE means at least some ganglioneuromas arise without passing through a neuroblastoma or ganglioneuroblastoma stage, against the proposal that all do. Both readings start from sympathetic neuroblasts, which the authors of the zebrafish study name as the common origin of ganglioneuroma and neuroblastoma; the question is whether a neuroblastic tumor comes first. The authors of the German NB97/NB2004 series note that a good part of ganglioneuroma has been hypothesized to originate from neuroblastoma, but that whether this holds for all ganglioneuromas, as Shimada proposed, remains unproven, and they read the rise of median age with the grade of differentiation as indirect support. A 2026 pathology review recounts that Cushing and Wolbach, comparing one patient's specimen at diagnosis with one resected 10 years later, reported that a malignant neuroblastoma could differentiate into a benign ganglioneuroma. In a 2022 case report, ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma at different sites of one patient shared the same molecular signatures, which the authors read as probable spontaneous differentiation from neuroblastoma to ganglioneuroma. In zebrafish, constitutively activated Akt2 in the sympathetic nervous system produced low-grade ganglioneuroma, and the fish did not develop neuroblastoma; this is a model of ganglioneuroma forming without a neuroblastoma stage, and its tumors had poor Schwannian stroma. The authors regard the model as faithful to human ganglioneuroma, which also shows AKT activation, but whether human ganglioneuromas form without a neuroblastoma stage has not been shown. The answer decides whether ganglioneuroma biology can be read as the end state of favorable neuroblastoma.
Posed 2026-10-28T00:00:00Z
Show evidence (6 references)
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"It has been hypothesized that a good part of GN originates from neuroblastoma. ... However, whether this is true for all GN, as proposed by Shimada ... still remains unproven."
In the discussion of this retrospective study of localized neuroblastic tumors registered in the German neuroblastoma trials, the authors restate the hypothesis, which they trace to Cushing and to later reports in stage 4S and localized neuroblastoma, that a good part of ganglioneuroma arises from neuroblastoma, and add that whether this is true for all ganglioneuromas, as Shimada proposed, remains unproven. The quoted sentences summarize earlier reports rather than the study's own data. The first sentence reports a hypothesis rather than asserting it, so it supports maturation from a neuroblastic tumor only at one remove, through the earlier reports the authors summarize (Cushing; stage 4S and localized neuroblastoma); the second records that the claim for all ganglioneuromas is unproven.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"An indirect support for this hypothesis can be seen by our observation that patients with GN and GNBI are older at diagnosis than those with immature NT. As Fig. 1 shows, the grade of neuroblastic differentiation increases with the median age at diagnosis. This is in line with the theory that..."
The same authors take their own age gradient as indirect support for maturation: patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, taken together, were older at diagnosis than those with immature tumors, which fits mature tumors having had time to mature in situ. The gradient compares different patients at diagnosis, and the paper reports no tumor seen to mature during follow-up; the authors add in the next paragraph that ultrasound check-ups of infants and young toddlers could find small immature neuroblastomas early, which bears on the same age difference without involving maturation.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"By comparing the same patient's specimen at diagnosis and a resected specimen 10 years later, Cushing and Wolbach reported that malignant NB could differentiate into benign GN [9]."
In its history of neuroblastic-tumor pathology, this 2026 review recounts that Cushing and Wolbach compared one patient's tumor at diagnosis with the specimen resected 10 years later and reported that a malignant neuroblastoma could differentiate into a benign ganglioneuroma. It is the review's account of a single human case it did not study, hence HUMAN_CLINICAL with REVIEW_SYNTHESIS; this review's classification-rule sentences, which report no patient data, are graded OTHER elsewhere in the KB. On the review's account a neuroblastoma matured into a ganglioneuroma in at least one patient. The sentence does not say whether this was spontaneous or followed treatment, and it says nothing about where ganglioneuromas come from in general.

Pathophysiology

5
Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
Differentiation of neuroblastic cells into ganglion cells. Biologically favorable peripheral neuroblastic tumors are considered to mature by age-dependent steps from neuroblastoma through intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma to ganglioneuroma. In German trials of localized tumors, median age at diagnosis rose with the grade of differentiation, which the authors read as indirect support for tumors maturing in situ. Whether every ganglioneuroma arises this way is unproven (discussion gn_origin).
differentiating neuroblast CL:0002676 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves differentiating neuroblast, annotated with neural crest derived neuroblast (CL:0002676). CL:0002676 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology. ganglion cell CL:0011103 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves ganglion cell, annotated with sympathetic neuron (CL:0011103). CL:0011103 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
maturation of neuroblasts into ganglion cells GO:0030182 Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves maturation of neuroblasts into ganglion cells, annotated with neuron differentiation (GO:0030182). GO:0030182 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"To note, tumors in the biologically favorable pNTs are considered to proceed by age-dependent differentiation/maturational steps from NB to GNB-i, and finally to GN."
This 2026 pathology review states that biologically favorable peripheral neuroblastic tumors are considered to proceed by age-dependent differentiation and maturation steps from neuroblastoma to GNB-intermixed and finally to ganglioneuroma, the maturation this node describes. The sentence sits in the review's account of the INPC categories and gives a consensus view ('are considered to') without supporting data, and it does not say that every ganglioneuroma arises this way.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"An indirect support for this hypothesis can be seen by our observation that patients with GN and GNBI are older at diagnosis than those with immature NT. As Fig. 1 shows, the grade of neuroblastic differentiation increases with the median age at diagnosis. This is in line with the theory that..."
In the Discussion of their German NB97/NB2004 series of localized neuroblastic tumors, the authors take their own age gradient as indirect support for the hypothesis that a good part of ganglioneuroma originates from neuroblastoma: patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, taken together, were older at diagnosis than those with immature tumors, and the grade of differentiation rose with median age, which fits mature tumors having had time to mature in situ before they were found. The support is indirect: the gradient compares different patients at diagnosis, and although residual tumors were followed over time, the paper reports no tumor seen to mature during follow-up. It bears on whether ganglioneuroma arises from an earlier neuroblastic tumor more than on how neuroblasts differentiate into ganglion cells. In the next paragraph the authors add that widespread ultrasound check-ups of infants and young toddlers in Germany could find more small, asymptomatic localized immature neuroblastomas in infancy, a point that bears on the same age difference without involving maturation.
Schwannian Stromal Development
The Schwann-cell stroma that dominates ganglioneuroma tissue; the INPC names ganglioneuroma for this feature (Schwannian stroma-dominant). Stromal Schwann cells in ganglioneuroma share the expression of nerve repair-associated genes with the repair Schwann cells of human nerve injured ex vivo, and repair-type Schwann cells and their secretome induce neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells in culture. It has been suggested that a minor increase in the volume of mature tumors may also be caused by proliferation of the Schwannian stroma rather than of tumor cells. Whether the stromal cells derive from the neoplastic clone is discussed in the Ganglioneuroblastoma entry.
Schwannian stromal cell CL:0002573 Cell Ontology (CL) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this cell type This pathophysiological event involves Schwannian stromal cell, annotated with Schwann cell (CL:0002573). CL:0002573 is a cell type from the Cell Ontology.
Schwann cell proliferation GO:0014010 Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves Schwann cell proliferation (GO:0014010). GO:0014010 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Downstream
Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation Repair-type Schwann cells and their secreted products induce neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells in culture; the evidence is in vitro.
Retroperitoneal Mass The tumor, composed largely of Schwannian stroma, forms a mass at its primary site; the retroperitoneum was the commonest site in a single-institution series.
Posterior Mediastinal Mass The tumor, composed largely of Schwannian stroma, forms a mass at its primary site; most thoracic ganglioneuromas lie in the posterior mediastinum.
Pelvic Mass The tumor, composed largely of Schwannian stroma, forms a mass at its primary site, which in some series is the presacral space.
Pain Symptoms come from the effects of the tumor mass; how the tumor produces pain is not described.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee names ganglioneuroma the Schwannian stroma-dominant category of neuroblastic tumors, so the tumor is defined by its dominant Schwannian stroma. This is a classification label: it says nothing about how the stroma develops or where its cells come from, so its support for this node is indirect. The quote keeps the abstract's spelling 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'.
PMID:33712610 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Indeed, stromal SCs in ganglioneuromas and repair SCs share the expression of nerve repair-associated genes."
The authors ran RNA-seq on fresh-frozen, Schwann cell stroma-rich areas of six diagnostic human ganglioneuroma specimens, collected at surgery or biopsy, in which S100B staining showed Schwann cells to be the prevalent cell population. They compared them with neuroblastoma tissue, which almost entirely lacks Schwann cells, and with human peripheral nerve fascicles, collected at reconstructive surgery, amputation or organ donation and left to degenerate ex vivo, a model in which Schwann cells take on the repair phenotype. After genes also expressed in neuroblastoma were excluded, the signature shared by ganglioneuroma and injured nerve contained nerve repair-associated genes, and the authors read this as stromal Schwann cells in ganglioneuroma taking on a repair-like state. This supports Schwann cells as the cell type of this node; it says nothing about Schwann cell proliferation. The profiles are of bulk tissue, so assigning the shared signature to the Schwann cells rests on sampling Schwann cell-rich areas and on subtracting the genes expressed in neuroblastoma. The grade reflects the ganglioneuroma side, which is patient tumor tissue; the injured-nerve comparator was a cultured nerve explant.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"An interesting question is why mature tumors can show progression at all. Nishihira et al suggested that a minor increase of tumor volume may also be caused by proliferation of Schwannian stroma rather than that of tumor cells [34]."
In the discussion of this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma in patients aged 21 years or younger at diagnosis, registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 neuroblastoma trials, the authors ask why mature tumors can progress at all. They restate a suggestion by Nishihira and colleagues that a minor increase in tumor volume may also be caused by proliferation of the Schwannian stroma rather than of tumor cells, and add that this could explain volume changes too small to count as progression under the International Neuroblastoma Response Criteria. This is the evidence from patients for Schwann cell proliferation (GO:0014010) in this node, and it is weak: it is a hedged suggestion restated from another group, not a result of this study, and the sentence does not say what the original observation was. No measurement of Schwann cell division in ganglioneuroma is cited, so an increase in Schwann cells by recruitment into the tumor is not ruled out.
Tumoral Vasoactive Intestinal Peptide Secretion
Secretion of vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) by the tumor, described in case reports of young children with ganglioneuroma and diarrhea. In one child with a retroperitoneal tumor, the tumor contained large amounts of VIP, plasma VIP was raised before surgery and the VIP concentration was normal after it; in a child with a thoracic tumor, the authors attributed the diarrhea to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumor; and in a 3-year-old girl with a thoracic paraspinal tumor, serum VIP was 314 pmol/l before surgery (normal less than 30) and 14 pmol/l 6 months after it.
vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) secretion by the tumor GO:0030072 ↑ INCREASED Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves increased vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) secretion by the tumor, annotated with peptide hormone secretion (GO:0030072). GO:0030072 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Downstream
Watery Diarrhea In the case reports the authors attributed the watery diarrhea to VIP secreted by the tumor, and in two children it stopped once the VIP excess was removed with the tumor.
Hypokalemia Hypokalemia was attributed to a VIP-secreting ganglioneuroma in a case report; the route from VIP to low potassium is not described.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:7376870 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The tumour contained large amounts of vasoactive intestinal polypeptide (VIP) and preoperative plasma concentration of this peptide was increased. Postoperatively the VIP concentration was normalized, and the diarrhea ceased. Analysis of catecholamine metabolites in urine was normal. The..."
Single case report of a 2 1/2-year-old girl with persistent watery diarrhea and a retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma. The tumor contained large amounts of VIP and plasma VIP was raised before surgery. After the operation the VIP concentration returned to normal and the diarrhea stopped, while urinary catecholamine metabolites were normal; the authors suggest the watery diarrhea was due to the VIP-producing tumor. This supports the tumor as the source of a circulating VIP excess. It is one case, the abstract does not say how much of the tumor was removed, and the tumor and an involved lymph node were made of benign ganglioneuroma cells, which the authors judged compatible with an originally metastasizing neuroblastoma that had matured late.
PMID:1211964 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The findings suggest that the diarrhoea in the syndrome of ganglioneuroma and bowel dysfunction is due to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumour."
Case report of a young girl presenting with diarrhea and stridor who had a thoracic ganglioneuroma, partially resected, whose tissue held an abnormally high VIP content by radioimmunoassay. From this case the authors suggest that the diarrhea of the syndrome of ganglioneuroma and bowel dysfunction is due to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumor, which supports tumoral VIP secretion. It is a single case, generalized by the authors to the syndrome. The abstract reports no plasma VIP and records abnormal urinary catecholamines and catechol metabolites in the same child.
PMID:2833214 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"The pre-operative serum VIP was 314 pmol/l (normal less than 30). Her diarrhoea stopped after the removal of the tumour. The VIP was 14 pmol/l 6 months post-operatively."
The authors' own case of a 3-year-old girl with a VIP-secreting thoracic paraspinal ganglioneuroma: serum VIP was 314 pmol/l before surgery, where normal is less than 30 pmol/l, her diarrhea stopped after the tumor was removed, and VIP was 14 pmol/l 6 months after the operation. The fall to a normal value after resection points to the tumor as the source of the excess VIP. It is a single case, and the abstract does not say whether the tumor tissue was assayed for VIP.
Tumoral Catecholamine Secretion
Secretion of catecholamines by the tumor, which some ganglioneuromas show. In a series of primary ganglioneuromas, plasma and/or urinary catecholamines were increased in 39%, and the authors concluded that ganglioneuroma may present with increased catecholamine secretion; in an adrenal ganglioneuroma, raised urinary catecholamines normalized after resection, which the authors took as confirming the tumor as their source. Secretion is not universal: the authors of an adrenal series concluded that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not produce catecholamines. In a chart review, raised urinary vanillylmandelic and homovanillic acid appeared to become more common with larger ganglioneuromas, and the authors advised a search for occult neuroblastoma when the values are extremely high.
catecholamine secretion by the tumor GO:0050432 ↑ INCREASED Gene Ontology (GO) Relation: this pathophysiological event involves this biological process This pathophysiological event involves increased catecholamine secretion by the tumor, annotated with catecholamine secretion (GO:0050432). GO:0050432 is a biological process from the Gene Ontology.
Downstream
Elevated Urinary Homovanillic Acid Homovanillic acid is a catecholamine metabolite excreted in urine. In a chart review of ganglioneuromas, raised values appeared to become more common with larger tumors, which points to the tumor as their source, though the authors report this only as a trend; they advise a search for occult neuroblastoma, which can be the source instead, when values are extremely high.
Elevated Urinary Vanillylmandelic Acid Vanillylmandelic acid is a catecholamine metabolite excreted in urine. In a chart review of ganglioneuromas, raised values appeared to become more common with larger tumors, which points to the tumor as their source, though the authors report this only as a trend; they advise a search for occult neuroblastoma, which can be the source instead, when values are extremely high.
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:11346873 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Levels of catecholamines in plasma and/or urine were increased in 39%. ... Ganglioneuroma may present with metabolic activity such as increased secretion of catecholamines and/or mIBG uptake."
Retrospective series of primary ganglioneuromas, meaning tumors that were ganglioneuroma at diagnosis rather than ones evolving from a differentiating neuroblastoma, in patients registered with but not enrolled in national neuroblastoma trials. Plasma and/or urinary catecholamines were increased in 39%, and the authors conclude that ganglioneuroma may present with metabolic activity such as increased catecholamine secretion, which is the claim of this node. The abstract does not say how many patients were tested, which catecholamines were raised or in which fluid, and it reports no values after resection, so the tumor is the source by the authors' reading rather than by a demonstration such as normalization after surgery.
PMID:35800596 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Following resection, catecholamine levels normalized, confirming the resected right adrenal ganglioneuroma as the source of the catecholamine excess."
Case report of an elderly man with an incidental adrenal ganglioneuroma of maturing type and mildly raised urinary catecholamines. The levels normalized after open adrenalectomy, which the authors take as confirming the tumor as the source of the excess. It is a single case, and an adrenal tumor that secretes catecholamines may carry an unsampled pheochromocytoma component, so the case does not show which cells secreted them.
PMID:24033606 REFUTE DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Isolated AGNs do not produce catecholamines and have CT imaging characteristics that can help in distinguishing them from other adrenal and para-adrenal neoplasms."
Conclusion of a retrospective series of adrenal ganglioneuromas (AGNs) at a tertiary referral cancer center, in which catecholamine levels were normal in every patient with results. The authors conclude that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not produce catecholamines, which contradicts this node for tumors at that site. 'Isolated' most likely means without a pheochromocytoma component, the adrenal case above shows that the conclusion is not absolute, and the second half of the sentence, on CT features, is not used here.
AKT-mTOR-S6 Pathway Activation
Activation of the kinase AKT and of its downstream effectors mTOR and ribosomal protein S6 in ganglioneuroma tissue. In archival tumors from one hospital, immunohistochemistry detected phosphorylated AKT (Ser473) in 10 of 11 ganglioneuromas, a panel that included two ganglioneuroblastomas, against 1 of 15 poorly differentiated neuroblastomas, and phosphorylated mTOR and S6 in 9 and 10 of the 11, against 5 and 7 of the 15. In zebrafish, constitutively activated myr-Akt2 expressed in the sympathetic nervous system produced ganglioneuroma without progression to neuroblastoma, inducing sympathoadrenal lineage hyperplasia through increased cell proliferation, with activation of mTOR. The authors note that AKT activation in a tumor may be a consequence rather than a cause of its growth, and that the upstream signals activating AKT in human ganglioneuroma are not yet identified.

PMID:32728700 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Here, we found that phosphorylated, activated AKT and the downstream effectors mTOR and ribosomal protein S6 were more frequently detected in human primary ganglioneuromas than in poorly differentiated human neuroblastomas."
The authors summarize their immunohistochemistry of archival human tumors from Boston Children's Hospital: phosphorylated, activated AKT and its downstream effectors mTOR and ribosomal protein S6 were detected more often in primary ganglioneuromas than in poorly differentiated neuroblastomas. This is the human evidence for the node. The comparison group is poorly differentiated neuroblastoma only, and the samples were those available with sufficient quality for immunohistochemistry.
PMID:32728700 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"To understand the contribution of AKT to human primary neuroblastic tumors, we performed immunohistochemistry (IHC) for phosphorylated AKT on a panel of human primary ganglioneuromas (including two ganglioneuroblastoma samples) and compared these results with those from human primary..."
Phosphorylated AKT (Ser473) was moderate to high in 10 of 11 tumors of the ganglioneuroma panel, against detectable staining in 1 of 15 poorly differentiated neuroblastomas (P < 0.0001); the ellipses skip the bracketed confidence intervals. The authors state that the panel included two ganglioneuroblastoma samples, so the 11 are not all ganglioneuromas. Total AKT was present in every tumor of the panel, so the difference lies in its phosphorylation. A section counted as positive when more than 25% of viable tumor cells stained.
PMID:32728700 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"We found that phosphorylated mTOR (Ser2448) was expressed in 9 of 11 ganglioneuromas (81.82%; 95% CI ... but only in 5 of 15 poorly differentiated neuroblastomas (33.33%; 95% CI ... P = 0.0214; Fig. 1 B and Fig. S1 A). Similarly, phosphorylated S6 ribosomal protein (Ser235/236) was expressed in..."
In the same panel, phosphorylated mTOR (Ser2448) was found in 9 of 11 ganglioneuromas against 5 of 15 poorly differentiated neuroblastomas (P = 0.0214), and phosphorylated S6 ribosomal protein (Ser235/236) in 10 of 11 against 7 of 15 (P = 0.0362); the ellipses skip the bracketed confidence intervals. This supports activation downstream of AKT, though both markers were also found in some neuroblastomas (5 and 7 of 15), and the 11 tumors include two ganglioneuroblastomas.

Histopathology

3
Ganglion Cells in a Schwannian Stroma-Dominant Background
Ganglion cells distributed singly or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma; in mature ganglioneuroma they are all completely mature, and in maturing ganglioneuroma mature and maturing ganglion cells are mixed. Completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells. Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are incorporated into the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells. Grossly the tumors are elastic, yellowish white, well circumscribed and encapsulated, although deeply seated tumors may grow into the surrounding fat, without prognostic significance. The INPC classifies ganglioneuroma as its Schwannian stroma-dominant category and always places it in the favorable histology group.
Show evidence (4 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"Tumors in this category represent the most mature form of pNTs. They are elastic and yellowish white, and free surfaces are well-circumscribed and encapsulated. However, infiltrative growth into the surrounding fatty tissue, without prognostic significance, may be observed in deeply seated GN...."
In this 2026 pathology review these sentences open the paragraph headed 'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)', a heading the cached text drops; the quote itself refers to deeply seated GN, mature GN and maturing GN, and cites Fig. 2C, the ganglioneuroma panel. The review calls these tumors the most mature form of peripheral neuroblastic tumor and describes them as elastic, yellowish white, well circumscribed and encapsulated, with infiltration of the surrounding fat in deeply seated tumors that has no prognostic significance. It places the ganglion cells singly or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma, defines mature GN by completely mature ganglion cells and maturing GN by a mixture of mature and maturing ganglion cells, and states that completely mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells. This restates the INPC morphologic criteria rather than reporting case data.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so that naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells."
This sentence comes from the 2026 review's paragraph headed 'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)' (the cached text drops the heading), directly after the definitions of mature and maturing GN, and the review's Fig. 2C ganglioneuroma legend says the same. Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells. This restates an INPC morphologic criterion and reports no case data.
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee, whose six member pathologists defined the morphologic criteria from a review of 227 cases, classifies neuroblastic tumors into four categories. Ganglioneuroma is the third, the Schwannian stroma-dominant category, with maturing and mature subtypes; neuroblastoma is the stroma-poor category and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma the stroma-rich one. Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue, so ganglioneuroma is the only category whose label is stroma-dominant alone. The abstract prints the composite label as 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant', and the quote keeps that spelling. The abstract does not assign ganglioneuroma to a prognostic group; the classification itself is reported in the companion article (PMID:10421273).
+ 1 more reference
PHOX2B Immunoreactivity
PHOX2B was expressed in every ganglioneuroma examined in two immunohistochemistry series: diffusely in all 65 peripheral neuroblastic tumors of a pediatric series, which included 10 ganglioneuromas, with less intensity in differentiating neuroblastoma, ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma than in undifferentiated and poorly differentiated neuroblastoma, and in all 8 ganglioneuromas of a series of 77 autonomic nervous system tumors. A third series, which did not report ganglioneuromas separately, found PHOX2B in 92% of 76 peripheral neuroblastic tumors, including two of four adult cases, so the marker is not positive in every tumor of the family.
Show evidence (3 references)

"Phox2b was expressed diffusely in pNT (n = 65 of 65), strongly in NB-UD and NB-PD and with less intensity in NB-D, GNB and GN."
In this pediatric immunohistochemistry series, the 65 peripheral neuroblastic tumors (pNT) included 10 ganglioneuromas (GN), alongside undifferentiated (NB-UD), poorly differentiated (NB-PD) and differentiating (NB-D) neuroblastomas and ganglioneuroblastomas (GNB). Phox2b was expressed diffusely in all 65 pNT: strongly in NB-UD and NB-PD, and with less intensity in NB-D, GNB and GN. The sentence directly supports PHOX2B expression in ganglioneuroma, as in every pNT tested in this series, at lower intensity than in undifferentiated and poorly differentiated neuroblastoma. The abstract does not say whether the staining was nuclear or which ganglioneuroma cells stained, and the ganglioneuroma group is 10 tumors.
PMID:23715162 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical

Immunohistochemistry on 77 autonomic nervous system tumors, including 8 ganglioneuromas, found Phox2b expression in every neuroblastoma, ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma tested. This second series, by a different author group from PMID:28986989, supports PHOX2B expression in ganglioneuroma and in every neuroblastic tumor it examined. It reports no staining intensity, does not describe the staining as nuclear and gives no patient ages, and the ganglioneuroma group is 8 tumors. PMID:28640941 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"PHOX2B was positive in 70 (92%) peripheral neuroblastic tumours, including 68 of 72 (94%) paediatric and two of four (50%) adult cases."
In a series of 76 peripheral neuroblastic tumors (median age 2 years, four adults) stained with a rabbit monoclonal antibody, positivity was defined as nuclear immunoreactivity in at least 5% of cells, and PHOX2B was positive in 70 (92%), including 68 of 72 pediatric and two of four adult cases. The abstract does not report ganglioneuromas separately, so it bears on this finding only through the tumor family as a whole; it shows that PHOX2B is not positive in every peripheral neuroblastic tumor.
Tyrosine Hydroxylase Immunoreactivity
In a pediatric immunohistochemistry series, tyrosine hydroxylase staining was diffuse in every ganglioneuroma, as in all poorly differentiated and differentiating neuroblastomas and ganglioneuroblastomas, whereas nine of 15 undifferentiated neuroblastomas and a nodule in a nodular ganglioneuroblastoma did not express it.

"Diffuse TH was seen in all NB-PD, NB-D, GNB and GN, but nine of 15 NB-UD and a nodule in GNBn did not express TH (n = 55 of 65)."
In the same pediatric series, which included 10 ganglioneuromas (GN), diffuse tyrosine hydroxylase (TH) staining was seen in all poorly differentiated and differentiating neuroblastomas, ganglioneuroblastomas and ganglioneuromas, while nine of 15 undifferentiated neuroblastomas and a nodule in a nodular ganglioneuroblastoma did not express TH. The sentence directly supports diffuse TH staining in ganglioneuroma. The ganglioneuroma group is 10 tumors, and the abstract does not say which ganglioneuroma cells stained.

Medical Actions

2
Surgical Resection
Category: Therapeutic Action: surgical resectionNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is surgical resection, annotated with Tumor Resection (NCIT:C164212). NCIT:C164212 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Tumor Resection NCIT:C164212
Platform: Surgery
In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, no ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, and incomplete resection leaving minor residuals appeared to be sufficient treatment; the 11 patients given chemotherapy, 2 of them with ganglioneuroma, showed no substantial effect, and the authors' conclusion that chemotherapy is not effective pools the two tumor types. Postoperative complications are common: short-term complications followed 43.1% of operations, most often neurological deficits (22.2%), in a 101-patient single-institution series; complications followed surgery in 11 of 51 patients (21.6%) with a resected retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma at a sarcoma center, one of whom died on postoperative day 8 of bowel ischemia; post-operative complications occurred in 15 of 50 surgical patients (30%) in a pediatric series, and a UK nationwide series reported a 19% complication rate, both pooling ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma. In two children with a VIP-secreting ganglioneuroma, VIP returned to normal after the operation and the diarrhea stopped.
Mechanism Target:
INHIBITS Schwannian Stromal Development — Resection removes the stroma-dominant tumor tissue. In German trials of localized tumors, no tumor progressed after complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm; that this reflects removal of the stromal tissue is an inference, and the authors could not rule out that progression in large residuals came from immature components.
Show evidence (1 reference)
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"No progression was seen after complete resection or in patients with minor tumor residuals (<2 cm), neither in patients with GN nor in patients with GNBI."
In this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma (GN) and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) in children and young people registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, no tumor was seen to progress after complete resection or when the residual left behind was under 2 cm on MRI. This held for GN and GNBI taken separately, and every progression in the series arose in a larger residual. The sentence reports a clinical outcome and does not mention the stroma. Reading it as support for this link rests on resection removing the stroma-dominant tumor tissue, so it is indirect. The authors could not rule out that progression in large residuals came from immature components, and they cite a suggestion that minor increases in tumor volume may come from Schwannian stroma proliferation, so the quote does not show that stromal growth is what resection controls.
INHIBITS Tumoral Vasoactive Intestinal Peptide Secretion — Removing a VIP-secreting tumor ended the VIP excess in two children: after the operation VIP was normal, falling in one child from 314 pmol/l before surgery to 14 pmol/l 6 months after it, and the diarrhea stopped.

In a 2 1/2-year-old girl with a retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma, plasma VIP was raised before surgery, and after the operation the VIP concentration returned to normal and the diarrhea stopped, which links the operation to the end of the VIP excess. It is one case, and the abstract does not say how much of the tumor was removed.

It is a single case, and the abstract does not say whether the resection was complete.

PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Outcome was excellent and chemotherapy seems not effective while incomplete resection with minor residuals (<2 cm) appears to be sufficient for treatment."
This summarizes a retrospective study of localized tumors registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, including 162 ganglioneuroma and 55 intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma patients aged 21 years or younger. The authors conclude that outcome was excellent, that chemotherapy seems not to be effective and that incomplete resection leaving minor residuals under 2 cm appears to be sufficient treatment. Few patients received chemotherapy, most of them with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and the conclusion pools the two tumor types.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT INDIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Eleven patients received chemotherapy without substantial effect. ... Cytotoxic treatment was given to two patients with GN and 9 patients with GNBI. One patient with GN presented with a large stage 3 tumor and received chemotherapy because diagnosis of GN was made only from biopsy and immature..."
In the same German series, 11 patients received chemotherapy without substantial effect, and the Results show that only 2 of them had ganglioneuroma: one with a large stage 3 tumor diagnosed only on biopsy, in whom local physicians suspected immature components, and one with an intraspinal tumor mass. Neither responded significantly, and residual tumor was still being observed. The ganglioneuroma evidence against chemotherapy therefore rests on two patients; the other 9 had intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma. It bears on resection as the treatment only indirectly, by showing that chemotherapy was not an effective alternative in these patients.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"No progression was seen after complete resection or in patients with minor tumor residuals (<2 cm), neither in patients with GN nor in patients with GNBI."
In the same German series of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, no tumor progressed after complete resection or with minor residuals under 2 cm, in ganglioneuroma and in intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma taken separately; all five progressions in the series had residuals over 2 cm. The finding holds for this series and its follow-up, in which time to progression ranged from 3 to 80 months.
+ 4 more references
Watchful Observation
Category: Monitoring Action: watchful active observationNCI Thesaurus (NCIT) Relation: this treatment is this clinical intervention This treatment is watchful active observation, annotated with Observation Activity (NCIT:C25598). NCIT:C25598 is a clinical intervention from the NCI Thesaurus. Ontology label: Observation Activity NCIT:C25598
Observation of selected asymptomatic tumors. In a single-institution series of 101 patients, 31 (mean age 32.3 years) were managed by observation, chosen mainly because the tumor lay close to major vascular or neural structures (74.2%); none developed new symptoms over a mean follow-up of 90.9 months, reported as 90.9 ± 151 months, and two tumors grew minimally (0.08-0.17 cm/year). The authors concluded that observation is safe for asymptomatic tumors, particularly when adjacent to critical structures; the observed group was selected rather than randomized. A UK nationwide series of ganglioneuroma and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma concluded that watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with resection reserved for symptomatic patients, although a small proportion of observed patients had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery, and a Hospital for Sick Children series concluded that, because these tumors grow slowly and resection can carry significant morbidity, watch-and-wait approaches should be considered for some patients. Both series pool ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and in both a part of the observed group grew or went to surgery: in the UK series 21 of 58 observed patients (36%) required subsequent surgery, with progressive tumor growth given as a reason, and in the Hospital for Sick Children series 6 of 21 observed patients had progressive disease and 4 later underwent resection. The authors of a series of 51 retroperitoneal ganglioneuromas, all of them resected, regard active surveillance as a reasonable option for selected patients with small, asymptomatic tumors, particularly those with elevated surgical risk.

"Thirty-one patients were managed with observation (mean age 32.3 ± 17.7 years; mean follow-up duration 90.9 ± 151 months). Observation was chosen primarily due to proximity to major vascular or neural structures (74.2%). No patient in the observation group developed new symptoms, and 2 tumors..."
In this single-institution retrospective series, 31 patients (mean age 32.3 years) were managed with observation and followed for a mean of 90.9 months. The abstract reports this follow-up as 90.9 ± 151 months without saying what the ± value is; a spread larger than the mean suggests that follow-up length varied widely between patients. Proximity to major vascular or neural structures was the main reason for choosing observation (74.2%), which is a reason for the choice, not a comparison between tumors in different locations. No observed patient developed new symptoms, and two tumors grew minimally, at 0.08-0.17 cm/year. The abstract gives no imaging schedule, does not state whether the observed patients had symptoms when observation began, and does not say how their diagnosis was established.
PMID:42537227 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Observation is safe for asymptomatic tumors, particularly when adjacent to critical structures, with no growth observed on long-term follow-up, except for minimal growth in few cases."
This single-institution retrospective review covers 101 patients diagnosed with ganglioneuroma between 1995 and 2025. The authors conclude that observation is safe for asymptomatic tumors, particularly when they lie next to critical structures, with no growth observed on long-term follow-up apart from minimal growth in a few cases. This is their judgement about the 31 patients they observed, a group selected rather than randomized. The series did not compare observed tumors next to critical structures with others, so 'particularly' is the authors' recommendation, not a finding that observation is safer there. The abstract does not define safety; the outcomes it reports for the observed group are no new symptoms and at most minimal tumor growth.
PMID:39582110 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with surgical resection reserved for symptomatic patients. However, a small proportion of patients in the observation group had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery."
This UK CCLG nationwide retrospective review covers 259 patients with histologically confirmed non-metastatic ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (median age 62 months), 163 of them with ganglioneuroma. It concludes that observation can be safe, with resection reserved for symptomatic patients, though a small proportion of observed patients had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery. The two diagnoses are pooled and survival is not reported by management arm, so the safety statement is the authors' judgement. Twenty-one of the 58 observed patients (36%) later needed surgery, with progressive tumor growth cited as a reason.

Diagnosis

2
Histopathologic Classification
Ganglioneuroma is diagnosed and subtyped by histopathologic examination of tumor tissue under the INPC. A biopsy diagnosis is not always upheld at surgery: in a UK nationwide series of ganglioneuroma and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, 14 (12%) biopsy diagnoses of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma changed to neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical pathology, whereas in a Hospital for Sick Children series of the two tumors classification changed at resection in three cases and no ganglioneuroma was reclassified as neuroblastoma. A 2026 pathology review advises noting in the report that a favorable classification made on a core needle biopsy showing only intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma was determined on review of limited material.
histopathologic examination under the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification NCIT:C18190 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
Show evidence (3 references)
PMID:39582110 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"In 45 cases (39%), surgical pathology was different from pathology at biopsy, and in 14 (12%) the pathology changed from GN/GNBi to neuroblastoma/GNB nodular."
In this UK CCLG retrospective series of 259 patients with non-metastatic ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, surgical pathology differed from the biopsy diagnosis in 45 cases, and in 14 (12%) a biopsy diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma became neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma at surgery. The abstract gives no denominator for the percentages, does not split the changes by biopsy diagnosis or management arm, and does not say whether they reflect limited sampling, reinterpretation or change in the tumor before surgery.
PMID:29369484 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Pathology classification changed at resection for three cases, but no GN was reclassified to NB."
In this Hospital for Sick Children retrospective series (1990-2014) of 67 children and adolescents with ganglioneuroma (50) or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (17), the pathology classification changed at resection in three cases, which shows that a pre-resection diagnosis is not always upheld, but no ganglioneuroma was reclassified as neuroblastoma. It is a counterweight to the UK figure: in this series the changes did not turn a ganglioneuroma into neuroblastoma. The abstract does not say what the three changes were, whether the earlier diagnoses were made on biopsy, or how many patients had a biopsy before resection.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"especially when the samples obtained by PCNB show only GNB-i or GN histology, it is advisable to include a note of "Favorable Histology of this case according to the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification is determined based on review of limited material" in the surgical pathology report."
This 2026 pathology review advises, as the authors' routine practice, that when percutaneous core needle biopsy (PCNB) samples show only GNB-intermixed or ganglioneuroma histology, the report should note that the favorable INPC classification was determined on review of limited material. The preceding sentence gives the reason: a core needle biopsy can miss the neuroblastoma nodule of a nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. It is an expert recommendation about core needle biopsy, without case data.
Urinary Catecholamine Metabolites
In localized tumors registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials (patients aged 21 years or younger), urinary homovanillic acid was raised in 31.7% and vanillylmandelic acid in 19.3% of tested patients with ganglioneuroma, less often than in intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or in neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, so a normal result does not exclude the tumor. In a series of 27 adrenal ganglioneuromas at a referral cancer center, catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 patients with results. Among 45 histologically confirmed adrenal ganglioneuromas at one center, every tumor with an adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profile was a composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma, and the authors, finding that pure and composite tumors overlap on imaging, judged testing for catecholamine excess essential in any indeterminate adrenal mass.
urinary catecholamine metabolite measurement NCIT:C61044 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
urinary vanillylmandelic acid (VMA) LOINC:LP15947-2 Logical Observation Identifiers Names and Codes (LOINC) urinary homovanillic acid (HVA) LOINC:LP15096-8 Logical Observation Identifiers Names and Codes (LOINC)
VMA and HVA are bound at the LOINC Part level (Vanillylmandelate, Homovanillate), which names the analyte rather than one specimen or method code. The procedure is bound to Urine Chemistry Measurement because diagnosis_term takes one term and this row covers both analytes, as in the Neuroblastoma entry. The adrenal series cited here measured plasma or urine catecholamines or metanephrines, or did not say which.

Imaging Findings

1
MIBG Uptake OCCASIONAL
In localized tumors registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials (patients aged 21 years or younger), MIBG uptake was positive in 27 of 110 tested patients with ganglioneuroma (24.5%), against 22 of 36 with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (61.1%) and 398 of 444 with neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, reported together (89.6%).
Other
MIBG uptake on scintigraphy NCIT:C79553 NCI Thesaurus (NCIT)
The German series does not state the MIBG isotope, and NCIT:C79553 is specific to 123I-MIBG; OLS NCIT searches on 2026-10-28 for MIBG uptake and MIBG scintigraphy returned NCIT:C79553 as the only uptake finding, so NCIT has no isotope-neutral term (see also the Neuroblastoma entry's MIBG rows). Modality is OTHER because planar scintigraphy is not in ImagingModalityEnum. The frequency band rests on the ganglioneuroma column of the German series; the pooled mature-tumor figure in the same row (33.6%) would fall in a higher band. An adrenal series of adolescents and adults in which 131I-MIBG was negative in all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned (PMID:19781327) is cited under the Adrenal Gland Pheochromocytoma differential diagnosis rather than here, because a negative scan does not support uptake.

🌍

Epidemiology

3
Age at diagnosis
Ganglioneuroma is usually diagnosed in older children and adults. In German trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, median age at diagnosis was 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature ganglioneuroma, against 61 months for intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and 9 months for neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma taken together. Hospital series that set no age limit report a mean age of 26.9 years for 101 patients at one institution and a median age at diagnosis of 28 years (range 12-73) for 51 patients with a resected retroperitoneal tumor, and a single-center adrenal cohort pooled with a systematic review of published adrenal series gives a mean age at diagnosis of 39 years.
Show evidence (5 references)
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Human Clinical
"GN is recognized as a benign tumor usually diagnosed in older children and in adults (11) (12)."
The introduction states, citing earlier literature (references 11 and 12), that ganglioneuroma is a benign tumor usually diagnosed in older children and in adults. It restates prior work rather than a result of this study's CCG/COG cohort, which was pediatric and excluded mature ganglioneuroma.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Median age at diagnosis increased with grade of differentiation (NB/GNBN: 9; GNBI: 61; GN-maturing: 71; GN-mature: 125 months, p < 0.001)."
In this retrospective study of 808 consecutive localized neuroblastic tumors registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, in patients aged 21 years or younger with central histology review by INPC criteria, median age at diagnosis rose in order across the groups: 9 months for neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, which the paper reports only as one pooled group of 591, 61 months for the 55 intermixed ganglioneuroblastomas, 71 months for the 144 maturing and 125 months for the 18 mature ganglioneuromas. The abstract gives one p-value (p < 0.001) for the comparison, and no pairwise comparisons are reported in the text. Metastatic tumors and patients over 21 were excluded, so the 9-month figure is not the median age of neuroblastoma as a whole, and the ganglioneuroma medians leave out older patients.
PMID:42537227 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"A total of 101 patients (62.3% female, mean age 26.9 ± 15.9 years) were identified."
In this retrospective review of all patients diagnosed with ganglioneuroma at one institution between 1995 and 2025, the 101 patients had a mean age of 26.9 ± 15.9 years. The abstract gives no age range and does not say whether age was taken at diagnosis or at presentation. No age limit is stated and PubMed indexes the record for child and adolescent age groups, so this is not an adult-only series. It is one institution's case mix, not a population sample.
+ 2 more references
Sex distribution
The share of women differs between series: 62.3% of 101 patients at one institution and 33 of 51 patients with a resected retroperitoneal tumor, against 47% in a single-center adrenal cohort pooled with a systematic review of published adrenal series. These are hospital case mixes, not population sex ratios, and they do not establish a sex predilection.

Primary tumor site
In a single-institution series of 101 patients, tumors arose in the retroperitoneum (45.5%), the thoracic paraspinal or mediastinal region (32.7%), the presacral space (17.8%) and the cervical region (4%); that series does not report the adrenal gland as a separate site. In German trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 51 of 161 ganglioneuromas (31.7%) were adrenal, 67 (41.6%) abdomino-pelvic and 43 (26.7%) thoraco-cervical. In a separate surgical series of 22 thoracic ganglioneuromas, 19 lay in the posterior mediastinum.

⚖️

Clinical Burden

Low
Ganglioneuroma is benign and many tumors are found incidentally: 72% of lesions in a single-institution series of 101 patients were incidental findings, 70 of 161 patients aged 21 years or younger with localized ganglioneuroma in German trials (43.5%) were diagnosed by chance, and 72% of adrenal ganglioneuromas pooled from a single-center cohort and a systematic review were discovered incidentally. In the German trials no patient died of tumor progression; the one patient with ganglioneuroma who died had complications of surgery after complete resection. Treatment carries its own morbidity: a UK nationwide series reported a 19% complication rate and a Hospital for Sick Children series post-operative complications in 30% of surgical patients, both pooling ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma; complications followed surgery in 21.6% of 51 patients with a resected retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma, one of whom died of intestinal ischemia and necrosis, and short-term complications followed 43.1% of operations in the 101-patient series. Malignant transformation is rare: a 2000 literature review counted ten reported cases of malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumor arising in a ganglioneuroma, either de novo or at a site of previous irradiation, and the 101-patient series observed no malignant transformation during its follow-up.
Show evidence (10 references)

"A total of 101 patients (62.3% female, mean age 26.9 ± 15.9 years) were identified. ... Most lesions (72%) were incidental findings."
Retrospective single-institution series of patients diagnosed with ganglioneuroma between 1995 and 2025, published in a neurosurgical journal. Among 101 patients (mean age 26.9 years), 72% of lesions were incidental findings. The abstract counts patients rather than lesions and does not say what the 72% is a share of. Tumors arose in the retroperitoneum, the thoracic paraspinal or mediastinal region, the presacral space and the cervical region; no adrenal site is listed, so the case mix may differ from adrenal or childhood series.

Retrospective study of localized neuroblastic tumors registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials (diagnosed 2000-2009 at age 21 or younger, with central histology review). The text says diagnosis was made at routine check-ups or visits to the doctor for other reasons in 50% of all cases, with no significant difference between mature and immature tumors. Table 1 (the column headings and the Diagnosis by chance row) gives 70 of 161 patients with ganglioneuroma (43.5%), against 30 of 55 with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (54.5%) and 306 of 591 with neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma (51.8%); p1 compares ganglioneuroma with intermixed tumors (0.163) and p2 the pooled mature tumors with the immature ones (0.177). PMID:33721367 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"Overall, in the cohort study and systematic review, the mean age at diagnosis of AGN was 39 years, and 47% were women, Table 2. AGN were discovered incidentally in 72% of patients, due to symptoms of mass effect in 20%, and due to other symptoms in 8%."
Pooled descriptive total from a single-center retrospective cohort of 45 patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma (Mayo Clinic, 1995-2019) and a PRISMA systematic review of 14 published series, 421 patients in the pooled analysis. Across the cohort and the review, 72% of patients were discovered incidentally, 20% through symptoms of mass effect and 8% through other symptoms. The authors judged the data insufficient for meta-analysis, and mode of discovery was reported in only 10 of the 14 reviewed studies, so the 72% is a share of the patients in those series and the cohort, not of all 421; one of those series, a 50-patient cross-sectional multicenter study, recorded every case as incidental (Table 2). It covers adrenal tumors only. In the single-center cohort alone 76% were incidental, and its 20 composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma tumors were found incidentally less often than pure ganglioneuromas (65% vs 84%).

🔀

Differential Diagnoses

4

Conditions with similar clinical presentations that must be differentiated from Ganglioneuroma:

Ganglioneuroblastoma MONDO:0005035
Overlapping Features The next INPC category toward immaturity. Intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor neuroblastoma. A biopsy diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is not always upheld at surgery: in a UK nationwide series that pooled the two tumors, 14 (12%) such biopsy diagnoses became neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical pathology, whereas in a Hospital for Sick Children series, also pooled, classification changed at resection in three cases and no ganglioneuroma was reclassified as neuroblastoma. In German trials of localized tumors, ganglioneuroma less often took up MIBG and less often raised urinary catecholamine metabolites than intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma.
Distinguishing Features
  • Ganglioneuroma: neurites incorporated into the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, with no naked neurites identified around the ganglion cells; intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma: well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests
  • Ganglioneuroma has no stroma-poor neuroblastomatous component, which nodular ganglioneuroblastoma contains
  • In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, MIBG uptake was positive in 24.5% of ganglioneuromas against 61.1% of intermixed ganglioneuroblastomas, and ganglioneuroma less often raised urinary catecholamine metabolites
Show evidence (7 references)
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so that naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells."
This sentence comes from the 2026 review's paragraph headed 'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)' (the cached text drops the heading), directly after the definitions of mature and maturing GN, and the review's Fig. 2C ganglioneuroma legend says the same. It states that neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells. Under this differential it is the ganglioneuroma side of the contrast with the review's paragraph on intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, where neurites are segmentally naked within microscopic neuroblastic nests; the review describes the two categories in separate paragraphs and does not compare them directly. It restates INPC morphologic criteria and reports no case data.
PMID:41612478 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Other
"The tumors are characterized by the presence of well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells in various stages of differentiation with actively producing and easily recognizable neurites around their cytoplasm (Fig. 2B). Their neurites are segmentally naked in those microscopic nests and..."
These sentences close the paragraph headed 'Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich)' in this 2026 pathology review; the cached text drops the heading, and the quote itself says only 'The tumors'. The Fig. 2B legend they cite labels the image as ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed, with microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells and their naked neurites. They state that intermixed tumors are characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells at various stages of differentiation, whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests and are eventually incorporated into Schwannian cell cytoplasm in the stromal areas. The review's ganglioneuroma paragraph describes ganglion cells lying singly or in small clusters, with no naked neurites around them, and does not describe neuroblastic nests; the review describes the two categories in separate paragraphs and does not compare them directly. The quote concerns the intermixed category only, not nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, and restates INPC morphologic criteria rather than reporting case data.
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee classifies neuroblastic tumors into four categories: ganglioneuroma is Schwannian stroma-dominant, intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma stroma-rich, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue. The stroma-poor component therefore separates nodular ganglioneuroblastoma from ganglioneuroma. The quote keeps the abstract's spelling 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'.

Neuroblastoma MONDO:0005072
Overlapping Features The Schwannian stroma-poor end of the same INPC spectrum. Neuroblastoma is classified as favorable or unfavorable histology by an age-linked evaluation of its grade of differentiation and mitosis-karyorrhexis index, whereas ganglioneuroma is always of favorable histology. In German trials of localized tumors, neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, reported together, were diagnosed younger and more often took up MIBG and raised urinary catecholamine metabolites than ganglioneuroma.
Distinguishing Features
  • Schwannian stroma-poor histology in neuroblastoma, against stroma-dominant histology in ganglioneuroma
  • Median age at diagnosis 9 months for localized neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma taken together, against 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature ganglioneuroma, in German trials of patients aged 21 years or younger; these are group medians, not a cut-off
  • MIBG uptake positive in 89.6% of localized neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma against 24.5% of ganglioneuroma in the same series, a descriptive comparison with no test of ganglioneuroma alone against neuroblastoma
  • Urinary homovanillic acid raised in 61.6% and vanillylmandelic acid in 64.1% of localized neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, against 31.7% and 19.3% of ganglioneuroma
Show evidence (6 references)
PMID:10421272 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3)..."
The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee places neuroblastoma in the Schwannian stroma-poor category and ganglioneuroma in the Schwannian stroma-dominant one, the two ends of the four-category classification; its six member pathologists defined the criteria from a review of 227 cases. The quote keeps the abstract's spelling 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'.
PMID:19530234 SUPPORT DIRECT BACKGROUND Other
"NB (Schwannian stroma-poor tumor) is classified into FH or UH depending on age-linked evaluation of grade of neuroblastic differentiation and mitosis-karyorrhexis index (MKI). GNB-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) and GN (Schwannian stroma-dominant) tumors are always classified into the FH group."
In the introduction of a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology Group study of 552 ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma cases, Okamatsu et al. restate the INPC rules for the two ends of the spectrum: neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor) is classified as favorable or unfavorable histology by an age-linked evaluation of its grade of neuroblastic differentiation and mitosis-karyorrhexis index, whereas ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), like intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, is always of favorable histology. The sentences restate the INPC papers rather than reporting a result of the cohort.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Median age at diagnosis increased with grade of differentiation (NB/GNBN: 9; GNBI: 61; GN-maturing: 71; GN-mature: 125 months, p < 0.001)."
In German trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, reported as one group, were diagnosed at a median of 9 months, against 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature ganglioneuroma. These are group medians and the age ranges overlap: the paper's table of delayed surgeries includes a maturing ganglioneuroma diagnosed at 0-18 months. Metastatic tumors were excluded, so the 9-month figure is not the median age of neuroblastoma as a whole.
