Adrenal Gland Pheochromocytoma Not Yet Curated MONDO:0004974
Overlapping Features An adrenal ganglioneuroma must be told from pheochromocytoma (see the Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma entry), with which it can occur as a composite tumor. Pure and composite adrenal ganglioneuromas overlap on imaging, and an unrecognized pheochromocytoma carries perioperative morbidity, so the distinction matters before adrenalectomy. Catecholamine excess and MIBG uptake favored pheochromocytoma in an adolescent and adult adrenal series, but a minority of ganglioneuromas in German trials of younger patients took up MIBG or raised urinary homovanillic acid, so neither finding excludes ganglioneuroma.
Distinguishing Features
  • In an adolescent and adult adrenal series, catecholamine excess and MIBG uptake pointed to pheochromocytoma: 24-hour urinary catecholamines were raised in all 36 pheochromocytomas and normal in the 4 ganglioneuroma patients reported, and 131I-MIBG was positive in 25 (96.2%) pheochromocytomas and negative in all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned
  • Neither finding excludes ganglioneuroma: in German trials of localized tumors at any site in patients aged 21 years or younger, 24.5% of tested ganglioneuromas took up MIBG and 31.7% raised urinary homovanillic acid, a catecholamine metabolite
  • In another adrenal series catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 ganglioneuroma patients with results, and the authors concluded that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not produce catecholamines; a catecholamine-secreting adrenal ganglioneuroma has been reported
  • Among 45 histologically confirmed adrenal ganglioneuromas at one center, every tumor with an adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profile was a composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma
Show evidence (8 references)
PMID:19781327 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"Catecholamine levels in a 24-hour urine sample were normal in 4 patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma and increased in 36 (100%) cases with adrenal pheochromocytoma. (131)I-MIBG nuclear scan showed negative results in 4 patients (100%) with adrenal ganglioneuroma and positive results in 25..."
Retrospective series of 18 adrenal ganglioneuromas and 36 adrenal pheochromocytomas (1999-2007; ganglioneuroma mean age 41.2 years, range 16-67). 24-hour urinary catecholamines were raised in all 36 pheochromocytomas and normal in the 4 ganglioneuroma patients reported, and 131I-MIBG was positive in 25 (96.2%) pheochromocytomas and negative in all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned. The ganglioneuroma numbers are small: 9 asymptomatic ganglioneuromas under 4 cm had neither test, and the abstract does not say how many had urinary catecholamines measured. The result concerns adolescent and adult adrenal tumors. 'pheochromocytom' is PubMed's own spelling.
PMID:27465021 SUPPORT DIRECT PRIMARY RESULT Human Clinical
"| | GN (n = 162) | p1 | GNBI (n = 55) | GN / GNBI (n=217) | p 2 | NB / GNBN (n = 591) | ... | Positive mIBG-uptake ... | 27/110 | <0.001 ... | 22/36 | 49/146 | <0.001 ... | 398/444 | | 24.5 % | 61.1 % | 33.6 % | 89.6 % |"
Table 1 of the German NB97/NB2004 study of localized neuroblastic tumors (patients aged 21 or younger at diagnosis, all primary sites): MIBG uptake was positive in 27 of 110 tested patients with ganglioneuroma (24.5%). This qualifies the adult adrenal series above, in which all 4 scanned ganglioneuromas were MIBG-negative: uptake is uncommon in ganglioneuroma but does occur, so a positive scan does not by itself point to pheochromocytoma. The German cohort was not restricted to adrenal tumors, included no pheochromocytomas, and does not name the MIBG isotope.

Schwannoma MONDO:0002546
Overlapping Features In a pooled series of adrenal cases, most of them drawn from published reports, adrenal schwannoma and adrenal ganglioneuroma were similar except that ganglioneuroma patients were younger; all of the schwannomas and 92.5% of the ganglioneuromas were nonsecretory, so hormone testing does not separate them.
Distinguishing Features
  • Patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma were younger than those with adrenal schwannoma (28 vs 49.5 years) in a pooled adrenal series
Show evidence (2 references)
PMID:41563553 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"AS and AG were similar, except AG patients were younger (28 vs. 49.5 years, p = 0.003)."
This pooled series combines the authors' own adrenal schwannomas (AS) and adrenal ganglioneuromas (AG) with histopathologically confirmed cases from a systematic literature review; most of the cases come from published reports (25 of the 30 schwannomas and 25 of the 27 ganglioneuromas), so the comparison is graded as a review synthesis. The authors report the two tumors as similar except for age: patients with ganglioneuroma were younger (28 vs 49.5 years). The abstract does not list which features entered this comparison, or say whether the ages are medians or means, and it reports separately that progressive delayed enhancement occurred in all AS but not in all AG, so 'similar' should not be read as covering CT enhancement. The finding covers the adrenal site only.
PMID:41563553 SUPPORT DIRECT REVIEW SYNTHESIS Human Clinical
"All AS and 92.5% of AG were nonsecretory."
In the same pooled adrenal series, all adrenal schwannomas (AS) and 92.5% of adrenal ganglioneuromas (AG) were nonsecretory, so a nonsecretory mass does not separate the two tumors. The figures describe this selected, mostly published-case adrenal series only, and the abstract does not describe what the secretory minority of ganglioneuromas secreted.

Source YAML

click to show 
name: Ganglioneuroma
creation_date: '2026-10-28T16:12:00Z'
description: >-
  Ganglioneuroma is a benign peripheral neuroblastic tumor of the sympathetic
  nervous system and the most mature form in the family that also includes
  neuroblastoma and ganglioneuroblastoma. Its ganglion cells lie singly or in
  small clusters in Schwannian stroma, whose cells incorporate the neurites of
  the ganglion cells, so that no naked neurites are identified around them. The
  International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (INPC) makes
  ganglioneuroma its Schwannian stroma-dominant category, with a maturing
  subtype, which besides fully mature ganglion cells contains a minor component
  of scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, and a
  mature subtype, whose ganglion cells are all completely mature; the INPC
  always classifies ganglioneuroma as favorable histology. Ganglioneuroma is
  usually diagnosed in older children and adults. In German trials of localized
  neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 144 of 162
  ganglioneuromas (88.9%) were of the maturing subtype, and median age at
  diagnosis was 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature
  ganglioneuroma, against 9 months for neuroblastoma and nodular
  ganglioneuroblastoma taken together. In the cited hospital series the tumors
  arose most often in the retroperitoneum or elsewhere in the abdomen,
  including the adrenal gland, and next in the thorax, mainly in the posterior
  mediastinum; fewer arose in the presacral space or the neck, and many were
  found incidentally. Pain was the commonest symptom leading to diagnosis in
  the German trials, and watery diarrhea that the authors attributed to tumor
  secretion of vasoactive intestinal peptide has been described in case
  reports. In two immunohistochemistry series every ganglioneuroma tested
  expressed PHOX2B, and in one hospital's tumors phosphorylated AKT, mTOR and
  S6 were detected more often in ganglioneuroma than in poorly differentiated
  neuroblastoma. No initiating lesion has been described; MYCN amplification
  has been found in a few maturing ganglioneuromas. In the German trials urinary
  catecholamine metabolites were raised, and MIBG was taken up, less often in
  ganglioneuroma than in intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or in neuroblastoma.
  Treatment is surgical resection or, for selected asymptomatic tumors,
  observation. In the German trials no tumor progressed after complete
  resection or with residuals under 2 cm, chemotherapy had no substantial
  effect in the 11 patients who received it, 2 of them with ganglioneuroma, no
  patient died of tumor progression, and the one patient with ganglioneuroma
  who died had complications of surgery. The cited surgical series report postoperative
  complication rates of 19% to 43.1%, counted per patient or per operation and
  in two series pooled with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and one patient
  in a 51-patient series of resected retroperitoneal tumors died after
  surgery. Whether every ganglioneuroma arises by maturation of a
  neuroblastoma is unproven (discussion gn_origin).
categories:
- Benign Neoplasm
- Neural Tumor
- Solid Tumor
parents:
- neuroblastic tumor
synonyms:
- ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)
- GN
notes: >-
  This entry covers ganglioneuroma of the peripheral sympathetic nervous system
  in both INPC subtypes, maturing and mature. MONDO:0005033 also has two central
  nervous system parents, central nervous system primitive neuroectodermal
  neoplasm (MONDO:0000640) and mixed neuronal-glial tumor (MONDO:0016729), and
  one child, cutaneous ganglioneuroma (MONDO:0002293); NCIT files
  ganglioneuromatosis (NCIT:C66804) and skin (NCIT:C4481), choroidal
  (NCIT:C188956) and colorectal (NCIT:C96514) ganglioneuroma beneath
  NCIT:C3049. None of these is covered. MONDO defines ganglioneuroma as a tumor
  that occurs in childhood, but the cited series that include adults
  (PMID:42537227, PMID:41306794, PMID:33721367) show that it also presents in
  adults. Intestinal ganglioneuromatosis is a different lesion and is not
  modelled as a subtype: its generalized form is usually associated with NF1 or
  MEN (PMID:23512313), and in a literature review of 55 patients with MEN2B and
  gastrointestinal symptoms the histological finding of intestinal
  ganglioneuromatosis led to the diagnosis of MEN2B in 15 (PMID:27899172).
  Composite pheochromocytoma-ganglioneuroma is recorded under the adrenal gland
  pheochromocytoma differential diagnosis, not as a subtype. Several cited
  series pool ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma
  (PMID:39582110, PMID:29369484) or restrict it by age and stage: the German
  series covers localized tumors diagnosed in 2000-2009 in patients aged 21
  years or younger (PMID:27465021), and mature ganglioneuroma was not eligible
  for the CCG/COG studies and was excluded from the CCG/COG series
  (PMID:19530234). No node is marked as the origin node (genetic_context),
  because no initiating lesion has been described for ganglioneuroma: the
  authors of the AKT study write that little is known about the genetics of
  mature ganglioneuroma and that the etiology and molecular basis of childhood
  ganglioneuroma remain elusive, and they only propose that somatically
  acquired mutations or epigenetic alterations in the PI3K-AKT-mTOR pathway
  may initiate it in individual patients (PMID:32728700). MYCN amplification,
  found in 2 maturing ganglioneuromas of the CCG/COG series, is recorded as a
  biomarker, not as an initiating lesion. icdo_morphology is not asserted:
  MONDO records the ICD-O cross-reference 9490/0, a benign code, while
  Embryonal Neoplasm, the value the Neuroblastoma and Ganglioneuroblastoma
  entries use, is defined as cancer arising from embryonic tissue, and its
  meaning NCIT:C3264 is not an ancestor of NCIT:C3049, whose NCIT parents are
  Benign Peripheral Nervous System Neoplasm (NCIT:C213862) and Autonomic
  Nervous System Neoplasm (NCIT:C5112).

- name: Age at diagnosis
  description: >-
    Ganglioneuroma is usually diagnosed in older children and adults. In German
    trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21 years or
    younger, median age at diagnosis was 71 months for maturing and 125 months
    for mature ganglioneuroma, against 61 months for intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma and 9 months for neuroblastoma and nodular
    ganglioneuroblastoma taken together. Hospital series that set no age limit
    report a mean age of 26.9 years for 101 patients at one institution and a
    median age at diagnosis of 28 years (range 12-73) for 51 patients with a
    resected retroperitoneal tumor, and a single-center adrenal cohort pooled
    with a systematic review of published adrenal series gives a mean age at
    diagnosis of 39 years.

      (the table does not say why one is missing): 51 were adrenal (31.7%), 67
      abdomino-pelvic (41.6%) and 43 thoraco-cervical (26.7%). The other columns
      are intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, the pooled mature tumors, and
      neuroblastoma with nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. The abdomino-pelvic row
      is not split into abdominal, pelvic or retroperitoneal sites, and the
      thoraco-cervical row counts thoracic and neck tumors together. The
      distribution describes this pediatric registry cohort, not ganglioneuroma
      at all ages.
  - reference: PMID:22573298

clinical_burden:
  burden_level: LOW
  rationale: >-
    Ganglioneuroma is benign and many tumors are found incidentally: 72% of
    lesions in a single-institution series of 101 patients were incidental
    findings, 70 of 161 patients aged 21 years or younger with localized
    ganglioneuroma in German trials (43.5%) were diagnosed by chance, and 72% of
    adrenal ganglioneuromas pooled from a single-center cohort and a systematic
    review were discovered incidentally. In the German trials no patient died of
    tumor progression; the one patient with ganglioneuroma who died had
    complications of surgery after complete resection. Treatment carries its
    own morbidity: a UK nationwide series reported a 19% complication rate and
    a Hospital for Sick Children series post-operative complications in 30% of
    surgical patients, both pooling ganglioneuroma with intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma; complications followed surgery in 21.6% of 51 patients
    with a resected retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma, one of whom died of
    intestinal ischemia and necrosis, and short-term complications followed
    43.1% of operations in the 101-patient series. Malignant transformation is
    rare: a 2000 literature review counted ten reported cases of malignant
    peripheral nerve sheath tumor arising in a ganglioneuroma, either de novo
    or at a site of previous irradiation, and the 101-patient series observed
    no malignant transformation during its follow-up.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:42537227
    reference_title: "A comprehensive analysis of ganglioneuromas: a 30-year experience of 101 patients."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "A total of 101 patients (62.3% female, mean age 26.9 ± 15.9 years) were identified. ... Most lesions (72%) were incidental findings."
    explanation: >-
      Retrospective single-institution series of patients diagnosed with
      ganglioneuroma between 1995 and 2025, published in a neurosurgical
      journal. Among 101 patients (mean age 26.9 years), 72% of lesions were
      incidental findings. The abstract counts patients rather than lesions and
      does not say what the 72% is a share of. Tumors arose in the
      retroperitoneum, the thoracic paraspinal or mediastinal region, the
      presacral space and the cervical region; no adrenal site is listed, so the
      case mix may differ from adrenal or childhood series.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."

      Retrospective study of localized neuroblastic tumors registered in the
      German NB97 and NB2004 trials (diagnosed 2000-2009 at age 21 or younger,

      routine check-ups or visits to the doctor for other reasons in 50% of all
      cases, with no significant difference between mature and immature tumors.
      Table 1 (the column headings and the Diagnosis by chance row) gives 70 of
      161 patients with ganglioneuroma (43.5%), against 30 of 55 with intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma (54.5%) and 306 of 591 with neuroblastoma or nodular

      (0.177). Chance discovery is therefore common in ganglioneuroma but not
      distinctive of it. Metastatic tumors were excluded.
  - reference: PMID:33721367
    reference_title: "Presentation and outcomes of adrenal ganglioneuromas: A cohort study and a systematic review of literature."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Overall, in the cohort study and systematic review, the mean age at diagnosis of AGN was 39 years, and 47% were women, Table 2. AGN were discovered incidentally in 72% of patients, due to symptoms of mass effect in 20%, and due to other symptoms in 8%."
    explanation: >-
      Pooled descriptive total from a single-center retrospective cohort of 45
      patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma (Mayo Clinic, 1995-2019) and a PRISMA
      systematic review of 14 published series, 421 patients in the pooled
      analysis. Across the cohort and the review, 72% of patients were
      discovered incidentally, 20% through symptoms of mass effect and 8%
      through other symptoms. The authors judged the data insufficient for
      meta-analysis, and mode of discovery was reported in only 10 of the 14
      reviewed studies, so the 72% is a share of the patients in those series
      and the cohort, not of all 421; one of those series, a 50-patient
      cross-sectional multicenter study, recorded every case as incidental
      (Table 2). It covers adrenal tumors only. In the single-center cohort
      alone 76% were incidental, and its 20 composite
      ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma tumors were found incidentally less often
      than pure ganglioneuromas (65% vs 84%).
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Two patients died of treatment related complications. One patient with GN died of surgery related complications after complete tumor resection in initial surgery. One patient with GNBI died of heart failure due to chemotherapy related pulmonary edema. However, no patient died of tumor progression."
    explanation: >-
      This retrospective analysis of the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and
      NB2004 covers patients aged 21 or younger with a localized neuroblastic
      tumor diagnosed between 1 January 2000 and 31 December 2009; 162 of the 808
      tumors were ganglioneuroma (GN) and 55 were intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI). Two deaths are reported, both from
      treatment-related complications: one GN patient died of surgery-related
      complications after complete resection at initial surgery, and one GNBI
      patient died of chemotherapy-related pulmonary edema. No patient in the
      pooled GN/GNBI cohort died of tumor progression, which includes every GN
      patient. The finding is limited to localized disease and to the study's
      follow-up: residual GN tumors had a median observation time of 42 months.
      Local progression did occur in three GN patients, all with residuals over
      2 cm, so the quote supports the absence of tumor deaths rather than the
      absence of progression.

  - reference: PMID:10755757
    reference_title: "Malignant peripheral nerve-sheath tumor arising in a previously irradiated neuroblastoma: report of 2 cases and a review of the literature."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT

    snippet: "Only ten cases of the rare occurrence of a malignant peripheral nerve-sheath tumor (MPNST) arising in a ganglioneuroma either de novo or at a site of previous irradiation have been reported."
    explanation: >-
      This case report and literature review, published in 2000, states in its
      background that only ten cases of malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumor
      (MPNST) arising in a ganglioneuroma had been reported, either de novo or at
      a site of previous irradiation. A count of reported cases is not an
      incidence rate; it supports malignant transformation being rare while
      showing that it does occur. The sentence restates earlier literature
      rather than the paper's own finding. The abstract does not say how many of
      the ten arose de novo. The paper's own two cases, additional to the ten,
      arose inside a benign ganglioneuroma 12 years after radiotherapy for a
      ganglioneuroblastoma and a neuroblastoma, so some reported
      transformations occur at previously irradiated sites rather than in an
      untreated primary ganglioneuroma. Ten is the count as of this review, not
      a current total.
has_subtypes:
- name: Maturing
  display_name: "Ganglioneuroma, maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant)"
  classification: histological
  subtype_term:
    preferred_term: maturing ganglioneuroma
    term:
      id: NCIT:C42064
      label: Maturing Ganglioneuroma
  subtype_frequency: VERY_FREQUENT
  description: >-
    In the INPC, maturing ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant) is
    composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component
    of individually scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing
    ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells; the fully mature
    ganglion cells are always covered with satellite cells. The INPC assigns
    tumors in this category to its favorable histology group. The subtype
    corresponds to the tumor group that the earlier Shimada classification
    called ganglioneuroblastoma, well differentiated. In German trials of
    localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, 144 of 162
    ganglioneuromas (88.9%) were maturing, with a median age at diagnosis of 71
    months; the very-frequent band describes that cohort. In CCG/COG studies,
    all 36 patients with maturing ganglioneuroma and a known stage had INSS
    stage 1-3 disease.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Ganglioneuroma-Maturing (Schwannian stroma-dominant): Composed predominantly of ganglioneuromatous stroma with a minor component of individually scattered, evenly or unevenly distributed differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells, in addition to fully mature ganglion cells. Fully mature ganglion cells are always covered with satellite cells. Tumors in this category are classified into a Favorable Histology Group."
    explanation: >-
      Table I of this CCG/COG clinicopathological series of 552 ganglioneuroma
      and ganglioneuroblastoma cases restates the International Neuroblastoma
      Pathology Classification definition of the ganglioneuroma-maturing
      subtype (Schwannian stroma-dominant). The tumor is mostly
      ganglioneuromatous stroma, with a minor component of individually
      scattered differentiating neuroblasts and/or maturing ganglion cells
      beside fully mature ganglion cells. The fully mature ganglion cells are
      always covered with satellite cells, and tumors in this category are
      classified as favorable histology. It is a classification rule restated
      from the INPC papers, not a result of this cohort.

    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."

      In the introduction of a Children's Cancer Group and Children's Oncology
      Group (CCG/COG) clinicopathological series, Okamatsu et al. restate the
      INPC subdivision of the ganglioneuroma category: GN-Maturing, which they

      sentence restates a published classification; it is not a result from the
      paper's own cohort.
  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology
      Committee, whose six member pathologists defined the morphologic criteria
      from a review of 227 cases, classifies neuroblastic tumors into four
      categories. Ganglioneuroma is the third, the Schwannian stroma-dominant
      category, with maturing and mature subtypes. The abstract prints the
      composite label of nodular ganglioneuroblastoma as
      'stroma-richlstroma-dominant', and the quote keeps that spelling. The
      abstract does not assign ganglioneuroma to a prognostic group; the
      classification itself is reported in the companion article
      (PMID:10421273).

  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In detail, 162 of 808 tumors (20.0 %) were classified as GN. The vast majority (n = 144, 88.9 %) of GN were subclassified as maturing subtype, only 18 GN (11.1 %) as mature subtype."
    explanation: >-
      Results of a retrospective analysis of localized neuroblastic tumors
      registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, restricted
      to patients diagnosed between 1 January 2000 and 31 December 2009, aged 21
      years or younger, with central histological review and INPC
      classification and a diagnosis made histologically before any cytotoxic
      treatment. Of the 162 tumors classified as ganglioneuroma, 144 (88.9%)
      were the maturing subtype and 18 (11.1%) the mature subtype, which places
      maturing ganglioneuroma in the very-frequent band and mature
      ganglioneuroma in the occasional band in this cohort. Patients over 21
      were not included, and the same study reported a higher median age at
      diagnosis for the mature than for the maturing subtype (125 vs 71
      months), so a series that includes adults may give a different split. The
      authors also note that individual pathologists might set the border
      between intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and maturing ganglioneuroma
      slightly differently, so that central review by one pathologist could
      shift tumors systematically towards ganglioneuroma or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma.

- name: Mature
  display_name: "Ganglioneuroma, mature (Schwannian stroma-dominant)"
  classification: histological
  subtype_term:
    preferred_term: mature ganglioneuroma
    term:
      id: NCIT:C42065
      label: Mature Ganglioneuroma
  subtype_frequency: OCCASIONAL
  description: >-
    The completely mature form of neuroblastic tumor: ganglioneuroma whose
    ganglion cells are all completely mature, in Schwannian stroma. Completely
    mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells, in either subtype.
    In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger,
    18 of 162 ganglioneuromas (11.1%) were mature, with a median age at
    diagnosis of 125 months; the occasional band describes that cohort, which
    left out older patients. Mature ganglioneuroma was not eligible for the
    CCG/COG studies and was excluded from the CCG/COG series of ganglioneuroma
    and ganglioneuroblastoma, whose ganglioneuroma data therefore cover the
    maturing subtype only.

      Committee, whose six member pathologists defined the morphologic criteria
      from a review of 227 cases, classifies neuroblastic tumors into four
      categories, the third being ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)
      with maturing and mature subtypes. The quote keeps the abstract's
      spelling 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'. The abstract does not assign
      ganglioneuroma to a prognostic group; the classification itself is
      reported in the companion article (PMID:10421273).
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "GNB-Intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich) and GN (Schwannian stroma-dominant) tumors are always classified into the FH group."
    explanation: >-
      Okamatsu et al. restate the INPC rule that ganglioneuroma (Schwannian
      stroma-dominant) tumors, like intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, are always
      classified into the Favorable Histology (FH) group. The rule is stated for
      the ganglioneuroma category as a whole and reaches the mature subtype
      through category membership; the CCG/COG cohort itself excluded the
      GN-mature subtype, because those cases were not eligible for the CCG/COG
      studies.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In detail, 162 of 808 tumors (20.0 %) were classified as GN. The vast majority (n = 144, 88.9 %) of GN were subclassified as maturing subtype, only 18 GN (11.1 %) as mature subtype."
    explanation: >-
      Results of a retrospective analysis of localized neuroblastic tumors
      registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004 (diagnosed
      between 1 January 2000 and 31 December 2009, aged 21 years or younger,
      centrally reviewed and classified under INPC, and diagnosed
      histologically before any cytotoxic treatment). Of the 162
      ganglioneuromas, 18 (11.1%) were the mature subtype, which places it in
      the occasional band in this cohort. Patients older than 21 were not
      included, and the mature subtype was diagnosed later than the maturing
      one (median 125 vs 71 months), so a series that includes adults may give
      a larger mature share. The authors note that the border between
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma and maturing ganglioneuroma might be set
      slightly differently by individual pathologists.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Median age at diagnosis increased with grade of differentiation (NB/GNBN: 9; GNBI: 61; GN-maturing: 71; GN-mature: 125 months, p < 0.001)."
    explanation: >-
      In German trials of localized neuroblastic tumors in patients aged 21
      years or younger, the 18 mature ganglioneuromas had the highest median age
      at diagnosis, 125 months, against 71 months for the 144 maturing ones.
      The mature-subtype median rests on 18 patients, and the abstract gives a
      single p-value for the gradient as a whole rather than a test of maturing
      against mature.
pathophysiology:
- name: Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
  description: >-
    Differentiation of neuroblastic cells into ganglion cells. Biologically
    favorable peripheral neuroblastic tumors are considered to mature by
    age-dependent steps from neuroblastoma through intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma to ganglioneuroma. In German trials of localized
    tumors, median age at diagnosis rose with the grade of differentiation,
    which the authors read as indirect support for tumors maturing in situ.
    Whether every ganglioneuroma arises this way is unproven (discussion
    gn_origin).
  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: differentiating neuroblast
    term:
      id: CL:0002676
      label: neural crest derived neuroblast
  - preferred_term: ganglion cell
    term:
      id: CL:0011103
      label: sympathetic neuron
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: maturation of neuroblasts into ganglion cells
    term:
      id: GO:0030182
      label: neuron differentiation
  notes: >-
    No node is marked as the origin node (genetic_context), because no
    initiating lesion has been described for ganglioneuroma: the authors of the
    AKT study write that little is known about the genetics of mature
    ganglioneuroma, and they only propose that somatically acquired mutations
    or epigenetic alterations in the PI3K-AKT-mTOR pathway may initiate it in
    individual patients (PMID:32728700). The MYCN amplification found in 2
    maturing ganglioneuromas is recorded as a biomarker in the genetic section.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "To note, tumors in the biologically favorable pNTs are considered to proceed by age-dependent differentiation/maturational steps from NB to GNB-i, and finally to GN."
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review states that biologically favorable peripheral
      neuroblastic tumors are considered to proceed by age-dependent
      differentiation and maturation steps from neuroblastoma to
      GNB-intermixed and finally to ganglioneuroma, the maturation this node
      describes. The sentence sits in the review's account of the INPC
      categories and gives a consensus view ('are considered to') without
      supporting data, and it does not say that every ganglioneuroma arises
      this way.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "An indirect support for this hypothesis can be seen by our observation that patients with GN and GNBI are older at diagnosis than those with immature NT. As Fig. 1 shows, the grade of neuroblastic differentiation increases with the median age at diagnosis. This is in line with the theory that mature NT might just have had enough time to mature in situ before they were discovered."
    explanation: >-
      In the Discussion of their German NB97/NB2004 series of localized
      neuroblastic tumors, the authors take their own age gradient as indirect
      support for the hypothesis that a good part of ganglioneuroma originates
      from neuroblastoma: patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma, taken together, were older at diagnosis than those
      with immature tumors, and the grade of differentiation rose with median
      age, which fits mature tumors having had time to mature in situ before
      they were found. The support is indirect: the gradient compares different
      patients at diagnosis, and although residual tumors were followed over
      time, the paper reports no tumor seen to mature during follow-up. It bears
      on whether ganglioneuroma arises from an earlier neuroblastic tumor more
      than on how neuroblasts differentiate into ganglion cells. In the next
      paragraph the authors add that widespread ultrasound check-ups of infants
      and young toddlers in Germany could find more small, asymptomatic
      localized immature neuroblastomas in infancy, a point that bears on the
      same age difference without involving maturation.
- name: Schwannian Stromal Development
  description: >-
    The Schwann-cell stroma that dominates ganglioneuroma tissue; the INPC names
    ganglioneuroma for this feature (Schwannian stroma-dominant). Stromal
    Schwann cells in ganglioneuroma share the expression of nerve
    repair-associated genes with the repair Schwann cells of human nerve
    injured ex vivo, and repair-type Schwann cells and their secretome induce
    neuronal differentiation of neuroblastoma cells in culture. It has been
    suggested that a minor increase in the volume of mature tumors may also be
    caused by proliferation of the Schwannian stroma rather than of tumor
    cells. Whether the stromal cells derive from the neoplastic clone is
    discussed in the Ganglioneuroblastoma entry.
  biological_scale: TISSUE
  cell_types:
  - preferred_term: Schwannian stromal cell
    term:
      id: CL:0002573
      label: Schwann cell
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: Schwann cell proliferation
    term:
      id: GO:0014010
      label: Schwann cell proliferation
  notes: >-
    The node is named for the INPC term Schwannian stromal development rather
    than for stroma recruitment, because recruitment presumes a non-neoplastic
    origin that is disputed (discussion gnb_schwannian_stroma_origin in the
    Ganglioneuroblastoma entry).
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology
      Committee names ganglioneuroma the Schwannian stroma-dominant category of
      neuroblastic tumors, so the tumor is defined by its dominant Schwannian
      stroma. This is a classification label: it says nothing about how the
      stroma develops or where its cells come from, so its support for this node
      is indirect. The quote keeps the abstract's spelling
      'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'.
  - reference: PMID:33712610
    reference_title: "Schwann cell plasticity regulates neuroblastic tumor cell differentiation via epidermal growth factor-like protein 8."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Indeed, stromal SCs in ganglioneuromas and repair SCs share the expression of nerve repair-associated genes."
    explanation: >-
      The authors ran RNA-seq on fresh-frozen, Schwann cell stroma-rich areas of
      six diagnostic human ganglioneuroma specimens, collected at surgery or
      biopsy, in which S100B staining showed Schwann cells to be the prevalent
      cell population. They compared them with neuroblastoma tissue, which
      almost entirely lacks Schwann cells, and with human peripheral nerve
      fascicles, collected at reconstructive surgery, amputation or organ
      donation and left to degenerate ex vivo, a model in which Schwann cells
      take on the repair phenotype. After genes also expressed in neuroblastoma
      were excluded, the signature shared by ganglioneuroma and injured nerve
      contained nerve repair-associated genes, and the authors read this as
      stromal Schwann cells in ganglioneuroma taking on a repair-like state.
      This supports Schwann cells as the cell type of this node; it says nothing
      about Schwann cell proliferation. The profiles are of bulk tissue, so
      assigning the shared signature to the Schwann cells rests on sampling
      Schwann cell-rich areas and on subtracting the genes expressed in
      neuroblastoma. The grade reflects the ganglioneuroma side, which is
      patient tumor tissue; the injured-nerve comparator was a cultured nerve
      explant.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "An interesting question is why mature tumors can show progression at all. Nishihira et al suggested that a minor increase of tumor volume may also be caused by proliferation of Schwannian stroma rather than that of tumor cells [34]."
    explanation: >-
      In the discussion of this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma
      and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma in patients aged 21 years or younger
      at diagnosis, registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 neuroblastoma
      trials, the authors ask why mature tumors can progress at all. They
      restate a suggestion by Nishihira and colleagues that a minor increase in
      tumor volume may also be caused by proliferation of the Schwannian stroma
      rather than of tumor cells, and add that this could explain volume
      changes too small to count as progression under the International
      Neuroblastoma Response Criteria. This is the evidence from patients for
      Schwann cell proliferation (GO:0014010) in this node, and it is weak: it
      is a hedged suggestion restated from another group, not a result of this
      study, and the sentence does not say what the original observation was.
      No measurement of Schwann cell division in ganglioneuroma is cited, so an
      increase in Schwann cells by recruitment into the tumor is not ruled out.

      supports: SUPPORT

      snippet: "Neuroblastoma cells, derived from aggressive tumors, respond to primary repair-related SCs and their secretome with increased neuronal differentiation and reduced proliferation."
      explanation: >-

        products, showed increased neuronal differentiation and reduced

      snippet: "Tumors in this category represent the most mature form of pNTs. They are elastic and yellowish white, and free surfaces are well-circumscribed and encapsulated.         category, the tumors are elastic, yellowish white, well circumscribed
        and encapsulated, and their ganglion cells lie singly or in small
        clusters in Schwannian stroma, so the mass is made largely of
        stroma-dominant tissue. Reading the Schwannian stroma as forming the
        mass is an inference, and the sentences name no site; where the mass
        lies is recorded on the phenotype. The passage restates INPC
        morphologic criteria and reports no case data.

    - reference: PMID:33721367

      supports: SUPPORT

        in their systematic review, adrenal ganglioneuromas were discovered
        incidentally in 72% of patients, because of mass-effect symptoms in 20%
        and because of other symptoms in 8%. The authors attribute the symptoms
        in that fifth to the tumor mass, which their introduction describes as a
        stroma of mature Schwann cells, ganglion cells and nerve fibers. Support
        for an edge to Pain is indirect: the paper does not say which mass-effect

        The pooled patients had adrenal tumors only, were mostly adults (mean age
        at diagnosis 39 years), and include composite
        ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma tumors.
- name: Tumoral Vasoactive Intestinal Peptide Secretion
  description: >-
    Secretion of vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) by the tumor, described in
    case reports of young children with ganglioneuroma and diarrhea. In one
    child with a retroperitoneal tumor, the tumor contained large amounts of
    VIP, plasma VIP was raised before surgery and the VIP concentration was
    normal after it; in a child with a thoracic tumor, the authors attributed
    the diarrhea to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumor; and in a 3-year-old
    girl with a thoracic paraspinal tumor, serum VIP was 314 pmol/l before
    surgery (normal less than 30) and 14 pmol/l 6 months after it.
  biological_scale: CELLULAR
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) secretion by the tumor
    term:
      id: GO:0030072
      label: peptide hormone secretion
    modifier: INCREASED
  notes: >-
    No cell type is bound, because the cited reports do not say which tumor
    cells secrete VIP. OLS GO searches on 2026-10-28 for "vasoactive intestinal
    peptide secretion", "vasoactive intestinal" and "VIP secretion" returned VIP
    receptor binding and receptor activity terms (GO:0031890, GO:0004999) but no
    VIP secretion process, so the parent process peptide hormone secretion is
    bound.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:7376870
    reference_title: "Vasocative intestinal polypeptide (VIP)-producing ganglioneuroma in a child with chronic diarrhea."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The tumour contained large amounts of vasoactive intestinal polypeptide (VIP) and preoperative plasma concentration of this peptide was increased. Postoperatively the VIP concentration was normalized, and the diarrhea ceased. Analysis of catecholamine metabolites in urine was normal. The findings suggest that the watery diarrhea was due to the benign VIP-producing neural crest tumour."
    explanation: >-
      Single case report of a 2 1/2-year-old girl with persistent watery
      diarrhea and a retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma. The tumor contained large
      amounts of VIP and plasma VIP was raised before surgery. After the
      operation the VIP concentration returned to normal and the diarrhea
      stopped, while urinary catecholamine metabolites were normal; the authors
      suggest the watery diarrhea was due to the VIP-producing tumor. This
      supports the tumor as the source of a circulating VIP excess. It is one
      case, the abstract does not say how much of the tumor was removed, and
      the tumor and an involved lymph node were made of benign ganglioneuroma
      cells, which the authors judged compatible with an originally
      metastasizing neuroblastoma that had matured late.
  - reference: PMID:1211964
    reference_title: "Watery diarrhoea and ganglioneuroma with secretion of vasoactive intestinal peptide."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "The findings suggest that the diarrhoea in the syndrome of ganglioneuroma and bowel dysfunction is due to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumour."
    explanation: >-
      Case report of a young girl presenting with diarrhea and stridor who had
      a thoracic ganglioneuroma, partially resected, whose tissue held an
      abnormally high VIP content by radioimmunoassay. From this case the
      authors suggest that the diarrhea of the syndrome of ganglioneuroma and
      bowel dysfunction is due to an excess of VIP secreted by the tumor, which
      supports tumoral VIP secretion. It is a single case, generalized by the
      authors to the syndrome. The abstract reports no plasma VIP and records
      abnormal urinary catecholamines and catechol metabolites in the same
      child.

        tumor VIP secretion to hypokalemia indirectly: the sentence attributes
        the hypokalemia to the tumor, which it describes as VIP-secreting, but
        does not say that VIP itself produced the hypokalemia or by what route.
        It is a single case.
- name: Tumoral Catecholamine Secretion
  description: >-
    Secretion of catecholamines by the tumor, which some ganglioneuromas show.
    In a series of primary ganglioneuromas, plasma and/or urinary
    catecholamines were increased in 39%, and the authors concluded that
    ganglioneuroma may present with increased catecholamine secretion; in an
    adrenal ganglioneuroma, raised urinary catecholamines normalized after
    resection, which the authors took as confirming the tumor as their source.
    Secretion is not universal: the authors of an adrenal series concluded
    that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not produce catecholamines. In a
    chart review, raised urinary vanillylmandelic and homovanillic acid
    appeared to become more common with larger ganglioneuromas, and the
    authors advised a search for occult neuroblastoma when the values are
    extremely high.

      label: catecholamine secretion

      Retrospective series of primary ganglioneuromas, meaning tumors that were

      raised or in which fluid, and it reports no values after resection, so
      the tumor is the source by the authors' reading rather than by a
      demonstration such as normalization after surgery.

  downstream:
  - target: Elevated Urinary Homovanillic Acid
    causal_link_type: INDIRECT_KNOWN_INTERMEDIATES
    intermediate_mechanisms:
    - Metabolism of the secreted catecholamines to homovanillic acid and its excretion in urine
    description: >-
      Homovanillic acid is a catecholamine metabolite excreted in urine. In a
      chart review of ganglioneuromas, raised values appeared to become more
      common with larger tumors, which points to the tumor as their source,
      though the authors report this only as a trend; they advise a search for
      occult neuroblastoma, which can be the source instead, when values are
      extremely high.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:8107654
      reference_title: "Catecholamine metabolites in ganglioneuroma."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "Even excluding this patient, there was a trend for elevated values in GN patients to correlate with tumor size (P = .07 and .14 for VMA and HVA, respectively). The incidence of elevated values appears to increase as a function of tumor size, and small tumors are not likely to result in positive urinary measurements. We conclude that while elevations of VMA and HVA are consistent with a well-documented diagnosis of GN, extreme elevations (> 3 x nl) should prompt careful serial evaluation for occult NB."
      explanation: >-
        In a chart review of patients with ganglioneuroma (GN), raised urinary
        vanillylmandelic acid (VMA) and homovanillic acid (HVA) tended to go
        with larger tumors, even after excluding the patient whose later
        resection disclosed foci of neuroblastoma (NB), and the authors
        conclude that small tumors are unlikely to give positive urinary
        results. Raised values becoming more common with tumor size points to
        the tumor as the source of the urinary HVA, which is the inference
        this edge rests on, and the authors report the HVA association only as
        a trend (P = .14). They also conclude that raised VMA and HVA are
        consistent with a well-documented ganglioneuroma, but that extreme
        elevations should prompt a search for occult neuroblastoma, which may
        then be their source.

- name: AKT-mTOR-S6 Pathway Activation
  description: >-
    Activation of the kinase AKT and of its downstream effectors mTOR and
    ribosomal protein S6 in ganglioneuroma tissue. In archival tumors from one
    hospital, immunohistochemistry detected phosphorylated AKT (Ser473) in 10 of
    11 ganglioneuromas, a panel that included two ganglioneuroblastomas, against
    1 of 15 poorly differentiated neuroblastomas, and phosphorylated mTOR and S6
    in 9 and 10 of the 11, against 5 and 7 of the 15. In zebrafish,
    constitutively activated myr-Akt2 expressed in the sympathetic nervous
    system produced ganglioneuroma without progression to neuroblastoma,
    inducing sympathoadrenal lineage hyperplasia through increased cell
    proliferation, with activation of mTOR. The authors note that AKT activation
    in a tumor may be a consequence rather than a cause of its growth, and that
    the upstream signals activating AKT in human ganglioneuroma are not yet
    identified.
  biological_scale: MOLECULAR
  biological_processes:
  - preferred_term: AKT signaling, read as phosphorylated AKT (Ser473)
    term:
      id: GO:0043491
      label: phosphatidylinositol 3-kinase/protein kinase B signal transduction
    modifier: INCREASED
  - preferred_term: mTOR signaling, read as phosphorylated mTOR (Ser2448) and S6 (Ser235/236)
    term:
      id: GO:0031929
      label: TOR signaling
    modifier: INCREASED
  notes: >-
    No genetic_context is recorded and the node is not an origin node: the study
    names no lesion that activates AKT in human ganglioneuroma, and its authors
    only propose that somatically acquired mutations or epigenetic alterations
    in the PI3K-AKT-mTOR pathway may initiate the tumor in individual patients.
    No cell type is bound, because the immunohistochemistry was scored in
    viable tumor cells without saying which cells stained. No downstream edge
    is drawn: the entry has no node for tumor cell proliferation, and only the
    zebrafish experiment tests whether the pathway drives tumor growth. Modifier
    INCREASED rather than GAIN_OF_FUNCTION, because the human data show the
    pathway more often phosphorylated, not escaped from its normal regulation.
    The mTOR inhibitors the authors tested in zebrafish are not entered as a
    treatment: no human ganglioneuroma has been treated in the cited work.

  - reference: PMID:32728700
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuromas are driven by activated AKT and can be therapeutically targeted with mTOR inhibitors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "Zebrafish transgenic for constitutively activated myr-Akt2 in the sympathetic nervous system were found to develop ganglioneuroma without progression to neuroblastoma. ... Mechanistically, myr-Akt2 overexpression induced sympathoadrenal lineage hyperplasia through increased cell proliferation, concurrent with activation of the AKT target mTOR."
    explanation: >-
      Transgenic zebrafish expressing a constitutively active, myristoylated
      murine Akt2 in the sympathetic nervous system developed ganglioneuroma in
      the interrenal gland, the fish counterpart of the adrenal gland, without
      progression to neuroblastoma; the transgene induced sympathoadrenal
      lineage hyperplasia through increased proliferation, with activation of
      mTOR. This shows that activated AKT is sufficient to produce a
      ganglioneuroma-like tumor in fish, which supports the pathway's
      relevance, not its activation in human tumors, so the support is
      indirect. The transgene is a forced, constitutively active kinase rather
      than a lesion found in patients, and the fish tumors had poor Schwannian
      stroma.
histopathology:
- name: Ganglion Cells in a Schwannian Stroma-Dominant Background
  finding_term:
    preferred_term: Schwannian stroma-dominant ganglioneuromatous tissue with mature or maturing ganglion cells
    term:
      id: NCIT:C42063
      label: Schwannian Stroma Predominant Component of the Tumor Volume
  description: >-
    Ganglion cells distributed singly or in small clusters in Schwannian stroma;
    in mature ganglioneuroma they are all completely mature, and in maturing
    ganglioneuroma mature and maturing ganglion cells are mixed. Completely
    mature ganglion cells are covered with satellite cells. Neurites produced by
    the ganglion cells are incorporated into the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal
    cells, so naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells.
    Grossly the tumors are elastic, yellowish white, well circumscribed and
    encapsulated, although deeply seated tumors may grow into the surrounding
    fat, without prognostic significance. The INPC classifies ganglioneuroma as
    its Schwannian stroma-dominant category and always places it in the
    favorable histology group.
  diagnostic: true
  context: Tumor tissue classified by the INPC
  notes: >-
    NCIT:C3049 Ganglioneuroma names the neoplasm rather than a microscopic
    finding, so the finding is bound to the morphologic-finding term
    NCIT:C42063, a child of NCIT:C42062 Schwannian Stroma More than 50 Percent
    of the Tumor Volume, which the Ganglioneuroblastoma entry binds.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT

    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Tumors in this category represent the most mature form of pNTs. They are elastic and yellowish white, and free surfaces are well-circumscribed and encapsulated.     explanation: >-

      'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)', a heading the cached text

  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology
      Committee, whose six member pathologists defined the morphologic criteria
      from a review of 227 cases, classifies neuroblastic tumors into four
      categories. Ganglioneuroma is the third, the Schwannian stroma-dominant
      category, with maturing and mature subtypes; neuroblastoma is the
      stroma-poor category and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma the stroma-rich
      one. Nodular ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or
      stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor tissue, so ganglioneuroma is the
      only category whose label is stroma-dominant alone. The abstract prints the
      composite label as 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant', and the quote keeps that
      spelling. The abstract does not assign ganglioneuroma to a prognostic
      group; the classification itself is reported in the companion article
      (PMID:10421273).

  description: >-
    PHOX2B was expressed in every ganglioneuroma examined in two
    immunohistochemistry series: diffusely in all 65 peripheral neuroblastic
    tumors of a pediatric series, which included 10 ganglioneuromas, with less
    intensity in differentiating neuroblastoma, ganglioneuroblastoma and

      sentence directly supports diffuse TH staining in ganglioneuroma. The
      ganglioneuroma group is 10 tumors, and the abstract does not say which
      ganglioneuroma cells stained.

  description: >-
    In localized ganglioneuromas of patients aged 21 years or younger registered

    pain as the most frequent symptom leading to diagnosis (34.2%), ahead of a
    palpable tumor mass (9.3%) and reduced general condition (6.2%); pain led to
    diagnosis in 14.5% of intermixed ganglioneuroblastomas and 13.6% of
    neuroblastomas and nodular ganglioneuroblastomas. The figure is the share

genetic:
- name: MYCN Amplification
  association: Rare Somatic Amplification
  gene_term:
    preferred_term: MYCN
    term:
      id: hgnc:7559
      label: MYCN
  relationship_type: BIOMARKER
  variant_origin: SOMATIC
  notes: >-
    MYCN amplification is rare in ganglioneuroma. In a CCG/COG series it was
    found in 2 of the 34 maturing ganglioneuromas with a MYCN result, and both
    patients were alive and well at last follow-up; the authors suggest that
    amplified MYCN may not have an adverse prognostic effect in these rare
    favorable-histology tumors. An earlier Children's Cancer Group series found
    no amplification in its 9 ganglioneuromas. The two cooperative-group
    series may share patients, and mature ganglioneuroma was not eligible for
    the CCG/COG studies. No frequency band is set, because the two small series
    disagree (2 of 34 against 0 of 9). No cited source treats MYCN
    amplification as the initiating lesion of ganglioneuroma, so no
    pathophysiology node carries it and no node is marked as the origin node.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "| GN-maturing N (%) | GNB-intermixed N (%) | GNB-nodular FS N (%) | GNB-nodular US N (%) | ... MYCN status ... | Not Amp** | 32 (94%) | 174 (99%) | 53 (100%) | 231 (97%) | | Amp** | 2 (6%) | 2 (1%) | 0 (0%) | 6 (3%) | | Unknown | 9 | 22 | 6 | 15 |"
    explanation: >-
      Table III of this CCG/COG series of patients registered on neuroblastoma
      studies in 1991-2006, tabulating MYCN status by centrally reviewed
      histology; the excerpt gives the column headings and the MYCN rows. In
      the GN-maturing column, 2 of the 34 tumors with a result (6%) had
      amplified MYCN, 32 were not amplified and 9 were untested. The series is
      not population-based, MYCN was determined by Southern blot, PCR and/or
      FISH in the reference laboratories, and mature ganglioneuroma was not
      eligible for these studies.
  - reference: PMID:19530234
    reference_title: "Clinicopathological characteristics of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma: a report from the CCG and COG."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "As expected, there were only 4 FH tumors (4/210, 1.9%) that had amplified MYCN in our series of GN-M (2 cases) and GNB-I (2 cases), and all 4 patients were alive and well at the time of last follow-up. It is suggested that amplified MYCN may not have an adverse prognostic effect in these rare FH cases."
    explanation: >-
      In the Discussion of the same CCG/COG series, the authors note that only
      4 favorable-histology tumors had amplified MYCN, 2 maturing
      ganglioneuromas and 2 intermixed ganglioneuroblastomas, and that all 4
      patients were alive and well at last follow-up, and they suggest that
      amplified MYCN may not have an adverse prognostic effect in these rare
      cases. The suggestion rests on 4 patients, of whom the Results say 3 had
      more than one year of follow-up.
  - reference: PMID:11745206
    reference_title: "Histopathology (International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification) and MYCN status in patients with peripheral neuroblastic tumors: a report from the Children's Cancer Group."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "MYCN amplification was seen almost exclusively in tumors of the NB category, and no patients with tumors in either the GNBi category or in the GN category and only two patients with tumors in the GNBn category had amplified MYCN."
    explanation: >-
      This Children's Cancer Group series classified 628 neuroblastic tumors
      under the INPC, 9 of them ganglioneuromas, and tested each for MYCN
      status. Amplification was seen almost exclusively in the neuroblastoma
      category, and none of the ganglioneuromas was amplified. This supports
      amplification being rare in ganglioneuroma and largely confined to
      neuroblastoma; it does not show that amplification never occurs, and
      the ganglioneuroma group is 9 tumors.
diagnosis:
- name: Histopathologic Classification
  description: >-
    Ganglioneuroma is diagnosed and subtyped by histopathologic examination of
    tumor tissue under the INPC. A biopsy diagnosis is not always upheld at
    surgery: in a UK nationwide series of ganglioneuroma and intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma, 14 (12%) biopsy diagnoses of ganglioneuroma or
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma changed to neuroblastoma or nodular
    ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical pathology, whereas in a Hospital for Sick
    Children series of the two tumors classification changed at resection in
    three cases and no ganglioneuroma was reclassified as neuroblastoma. A 2026
    pathology review advises noting in the report that a favorable
    classification made on a core needle biopsy showing only intermixed
    ganglioneuroblastoma or ganglioneuroma was determined on review of limited
    material.
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: histopathologic examination under the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification
    term:
      id: NCIT:C18190
      label: Histopathologic Examination
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:39582110
    reference_title: "Management of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma Intermixed: A United Kingdom Children's Cancer and Leukaemia Group (UK CCLG) Nationwide Study Report."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "In 45 cases (39%), surgical pathology was different from pathology at biopsy, and in 14 (12%) the pathology changed from GN/GNBi to neuroblastoma/GNB nodular."
    explanation: >-
      In this UK CCLG retrospective series of 259 patients with non-metastatic
      ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, surgical pathology
      differed from the biopsy diagnosis in 45 cases, and in 14 (12%) a biopsy
      diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma became
      neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma at surgery. The abstract
      gives no denominator for the percentages, does not split the changes by
      biopsy diagnosis or management arm, and does not say whether they reflect
      limited sampling, reinterpretation or change in the tumor before surgery.
  - reference: PMID:29369484
    reference_title: "Characteristics and management of ganglioneuroma and ganglioneuroblastoma-intermixed in children and adolescents."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Pathology classification changed at resection for three cases, but no GN was reclassified to NB."
    explanation: >-
      In this Hospital for Sick Children retrospective series (1990-2014) of 67
      children and adolescents with ganglioneuroma (50) or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma (17), the pathology classification changed at
      resection in three cases, which shows that a pre-resection diagnosis is
      not always upheld, but no ganglioneuroma was reclassified as
      neuroblastoma. It is a counterweight to the UK figure: in this series the
      changes did not turn a ganglioneuroma into neuroblastoma. The abstract
      does not say what the three changes were, whether the earlier diagnoses
      were made on biopsy, or how many patients had a biopsy before resection.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "especially when the samples obtained by PCNB show only GNB-i or GN histology, it is advisable to include a note of \"Favorable Histology of this case according to the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification is determined based on review of limited material\" in the surgical pathology report."
    explanation: >-
      This 2026 pathology review advises, as the authors' routine practice, that
      when percutaneous core needle biopsy (PCNB) samples show only
      GNB-intermixed or ganglioneuroma histology, the report should note that
      the favorable INPC classification was determined on review of limited
      material. The preceding sentence gives the reason: a core needle biopsy
      can miss the neuroblastoma nodule of a nodular ganglioneuroblastoma. It is
      an expert recommendation about core needle biopsy, without case data.
- name: Urinary Catecholamine Metabolites
  description: >-
    In localized tumors registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials
    (patients aged 21 years or younger), urinary homovanillic acid was raised in
    31.7% and vanillylmandelic acid in 19.3% of tested patients with
    ganglioneuroma, less often than in intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma or in
    neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, so a normal result does not
    exclude the tumor. In a series of 27 adrenal ganglioneuromas at a referral
    cancer center, catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 patients with
    results. Among 45 histologically confirmed adrenal ganglioneuromas at one
    center, every tumor with an adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine
    profile was a composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma, and the authors,
    finding that pure and composite tumors overlap on imaging, judged testing
    for catecholamine excess essential in any indeterminate adrenal mass.
  diagnosis_term:
    preferred_term: urinary catecholamine metabolite measurement
    term:
      id: NCIT:C61044
      label: Urine Chemistry Measurement
  measurements:
  - preferred_term: urinary vanillylmandelic acid (VMA)
    term:
      id: LOINC:LP15947-2
      label: Vanillylmandelate
  - preferred_term: urinary homovanillic acid (HVA)
    term:
      id: LOINC:LP15096-8
      label: Homovanillate
  notes: >-
    VMA and HVA are bound at the LOINC Part level (Vanillylmandelate,
    Homovanillate), which names the analyte rather than one specimen or method
    code. The procedure is bound to Urine Chemistry Measurement because
    diagnosis_term takes one term and this row covers both analytes, as in the
    Neuroblastoma entry. The adrenal series cited here measured plasma or
    urine catecholamines or metanephrines, or did not say which.

    explanation: >-
      Results sentence from a retrospective series of 27 adrenal
      ganglioneuromas at a tertiary referral cancer center (median age 31 years,
      range 1.7-64): catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 patients with

  - reference: PMID:33721367
    reference_title: "Presentation and outcomes of adrenal ganglioneuromas: A cohort study and a systematic review of literature."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "All tumors with either adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profiles were found to be composite AGN-PHEO."
    explanation: >-
      Mayo Clinic single-center cohort of 45 histologically confirmed adrenal
      ganglioneuromas (1995-2019; median age 44, range 6-87), 20 of them
      composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytomas. Every tumor with an
      adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profile was composite (Table 1:
      15 adrenergic and 3 noradrenergic, all in the composite group), and all
      17 tested pure ganglioneuromas were non-functional. The finding holds only
      for tumors with ganglioneuroma histology; it does not mean that
      adrenergic adrenal tumors in general are composite. One composite tumor
      was non-functioning, so a normal catecholamine result does not completely
      exclude a pheochromocytoma component.
  - reference: PMID:33721367
    reference_title: "Presentation and outcomes of adrenal ganglioneuromas: A cohort study and a systematic review of literature."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Since imaging characteristics of AGN and composite AGN-PHEO overlap, evaluation for catecholamine excess is essential in any indeterminate adrenal mass."
    explanation: >-
      In this cohort, pure and composite adrenal ganglioneuromas did not differ
      in laterality, calcification, homogeneity, shape or appearance, although
      composite tumors were smaller (median 39 vs 57 mm). From this the authors
      recommend testing for catecholamine excess in any indeterminate adrenal
      mass, which makes catecholamine status, not imaging, the way to detect a
      pheochromocytoma component. The support is indirect, because the sentence
      is a testing recommendation rather than a measured difference, and the
      cohort classified tumors by plasma or urine metanephrines or
      catecholamines, so it is not specific to urine testing.
imaging_findings:
- name: MIBG Uptake
  modality: OTHER
  frequency: OCCASIONAL
  imaging_finding_term:
    preferred_term: MIBG uptake on scintigraphy
    term:
      id: NCIT:C79553
      label: Increased Uptake by 123-Iodine-Meta-Iodobenzylguanidine Scintigraphy
  description: >-
    In localized tumors registered in the German NB97 and NB2004 trials
    (patients aged 21 years or younger), MIBG uptake was positive in 27 of 110
    tested patients with ganglioneuroma (24.5%), against 22 of 36 with
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (61.1%) and 398 of 444 with neuroblastoma or
    nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, reported together (89.6%).
  notes: >-
    The German series does not state the MIBG isotope, and NCIT:C79553 is
    specific to 123I-MIBG; OLS NCIT searches on 2026-10-28 for MIBG uptake and
    MIBG scintigraphy returned NCIT:C79553 as the only uptake finding, so NCIT
    has no isotope-neutral term (see also the Neuroblastoma entry's MIBG rows).
    Modality is OTHER because planar scintigraphy is not in
    ImagingModalityEnum. The frequency band rests on the ganglioneuroma column
    of the German series; the pooled mature-tumor figure in the same row
    (33.6%) would fall in a higher band. An adrenal series of adolescents and
    adults in which 131I-MIBG was negative in all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned
    (PMID:19781327) is cited under the Adrenal Gland Pheochromocytoma
    differential diagnosis rather than here, because a negative scan does not
    support uptake.

differential_diagnoses:
- name: Ganglioneuroblastoma
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: ganglioneuroblastoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0005035
      label: ganglioneuroblastoma
  description: >-
    The next INPC category toward immaturity. Intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma
    is characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells
    whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests, and nodular
    ganglioneuroblastoma is a composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue
    with stroma-poor neuroblastoma. A biopsy diagnosis of ganglioneuroma or
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma is not always upheld at surgery: in a UK
    nationwide series that pooled the two tumors, 14 (12%) such biopsy
    diagnoses became neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma on surgical
    pathology, whereas in a Hospital for Sick Children series, also pooled,
    classification changed at resection in three cases and no ganglioneuroma
    was reclassified as neuroblastoma. In German trials of localized tumors,
    ganglioneuroma less often took up MIBG and less often raised urinary
    catecholamine metabolites than intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma.
  distinguishing_features:
  - "Ganglioneuroma: neurites incorporated into the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, with no naked neurites identified around the ganglion cells; intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma: well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells whose neurites are segmentally naked within the nests"
  - "Ganglioneuroma has no stroma-poor neuroblastomatous component, which nodular ganglioneuroblastoma contains"
  - "In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger, MIBG uptake was positive in 24.5% of ganglioneuromas against 61.1% of intermixed ganglioneuroblastomas, and ganglioneuroma less often raised urinary catecholamine metabolites"
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "Neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so that naked neurites are not identified around the ganglion cells."
    explanation: >-
      This sentence comes from the 2026 review's paragraph headed
      'Ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant)' (the cached text drops the
      heading), directly after the definitions of mature and maturing GN, and
      the review's Fig. 2C ganglioneuroma legend says the same. It states that
      neurites produced by the ganglion cells are immediately incorporated in
      the cytoplasm of Schwannian stromal cells, so naked neurites are not
      identified around the ganglion cells. Under this differential it is the
      ganglioneuroma side of the contrast with the review's paragraph on
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, where neurites are segmentally naked
      within microscopic neuroblastic nests; the review describes the two
      categories in separate paragraphs and does not compare them directly. It
      restates INPC morphologic criteria and reports no case data.
  - reference: PMID:41612478
    reference_title: "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: OTHER
    snippet: "The tumors are characterized by the presence of well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells in various stages of differentiation with actively producing and easily recognizable neurites around their cytoplasm (Fig. 2B).
