    explanation: >-
      These sentences close the paragraph headed 'Ganglioneuroblastoma,
      intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich)' in this 2026 pathology review; the
      cached text drops the heading, and the quote itself says only 'The
      tumors'. The Fig. 2B legend they cite labels the image as
      ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed, with microscopic nests of neuroblastic
      cells and their naked neurites. They state that intermixed tumors are
      characterized by well-defined microscopic nests of neuroblastic cells at
      various stages of differentiation, whose neurites are segmentally naked
      within the nests and are eventually incorporated into Schwannian cell
      cytoplasm in the stromal areas. The review's ganglioneuroma paragraph
      describes ganglion cells lying singly or in small clusters, with no naked
      neurites around them, and does not describe neuroblastic nests; the
      review describes the two categories in separate paragraphs and does not
      compare them directly. The quote concerns the intermixed category only,
      not nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, and restates INPC morphologic criteria
      rather than reporting case data.
  - reference: PMID:10421272
    reference_title: "Terminology and morphologic criteria of neuroblastic tumors: recommendations by the International Neuroblastoma Pathology Committee."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Based on morphologic criteria defined in this article, NTs were classified into four categories and their subtypes: 1) neuroblastoma (Schwannian stroma-poor), undifferentiated, poorly differentiated, and differentiating; 2) ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed (Schwannian stroma-rich); 3) ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant), maturing and mature; and 4) ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular (composite Schwannian stroma-richlstroma-dominant and stroma-poor)."
    explanation: >-
      The 1999 terminology paper of the International Neuroblastoma Pathology
      Committee classifies neuroblastic tumors into four categories:
      ganglioneuroma is Schwannian stroma-dominant, intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma stroma-rich, and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma a
      composite of stroma-rich or stroma-dominant tissue with stroma-poor
      tissue. The stroma-poor component therefore separates nodular
      ganglioneuroblastoma from ganglioneuroma. The quote keeps the abstract's
      spelling 'stroma-richlstroma-dominant'.

- name: Neuroblastoma
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: neuroblastoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0005072
      label: neuroblastoma
  description: >-
    The Schwannian stroma-poor end of the same INPC spectrum. Neuroblastoma is
    classified as favorable or unfavorable histology by an age-linked
    evaluation of its grade of differentiation and mitosis-karyorrhexis index,
    whereas ganglioneuroma is always of favorable histology. In German trials of
    localized tumors, neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, reported
    together, were diagnosed younger and more often took up MIBG and raised
    urinary catecholamine metabolites than ganglioneuroma.
  distinguishing_features:
  - "Schwannian stroma-poor histology in neuroblastoma, against stroma-dominant histology in ganglioneuroma"
  - "Median age at diagnosis 9 months for localized neuroblastoma and nodular ganglioneuroblastoma taken together, against 71 months for maturing and 125 months for mature ganglioneuroma, in German trials of patients aged 21 years or younger; these are group medians, not a cut-off"
  - "MIBG uptake positive in 89.6% of localized neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma against 24.5% of ganglioneuroma in the same series, a descriptive comparison with no test of ganglioneuroma alone against neuroblastoma"
  - "Urinary homovanillic acid raised in 61.6% and vanillylmandelic acid in 64.1% of localized neuroblastoma or nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, against 31.7% and 19.3% of ganglioneuroma"

- name: Adrenal Gland Pheochromocytoma
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: adrenal gland pheochromocytoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0004974
      label: adrenal gland pheochromocytoma
  description: >-
    An adrenal ganglioneuroma must be told from pheochromocytoma (see the
    Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma entry), with which it can occur as a
    composite tumor. Pure and composite adrenal ganglioneuromas overlap on
    imaging, and an unrecognized pheochromocytoma carries perioperative
    morbidity, so the distinction matters before adrenalectomy. Catecholamine
    excess and MIBG uptake favored pheochromocytoma in an adolescent and adult
    adrenal series, but a minority of ganglioneuromas in German trials of
    younger patients took up MIBG or raised urinary homovanillic acid, so
    neither finding excludes ganglioneuroma.
  notes: >-
    Bound to adrenal gland pheochromocytoma (MONDO:0004974), because every
    cited comparison is of adrenal tumors. The Pheochromocytoma and
    Paraganglioma entry is bound to pheochromocytoma-paraganglioma
    (MONDO:0035540), and OLS on 2026-10-28 gave MONDO:0004974 the parents
    sympathetic paraganglioma (MONDO:0021072) and adrenal medulla neoplasm
    (MONDO:0021237), not MONDO:0035540, so the row does not resolve to that
    entry by its term.
  distinguishing_features:
  - "In an adolescent and adult adrenal series, catecholamine excess and MIBG uptake pointed to pheochromocytoma: 24-hour urinary catecholamines were raised in all 36 pheochromocytomas and normal in the 4 ganglioneuroma patients reported, and 131I-MIBG was positive in 25 (96.2%) pheochromocytomas and negative in all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned"
  - "Neither finding excludes ganglioneuroma: in German trials of localized tumors at any site in patients aged 21 years or younger, 24.5% of tested ganglioneuromas took up MIBG and 31.7% raised urinary homovanillic acid, a catecholamine metabolite"
  - "In another adrenal series catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 ganglioneuroma patients with results, and the authors concluded that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not produce catecholamines; a catecholamine-secreting adrenal ganglioneuroma has been reported"
  - "Among 45 histologically confirmed adrenal ganglioneuromas at one center, every tumor with an adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profile was a composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytoma"
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:19781327
    reference_title: "Differential diagnosis and laparoscopic treatment of adrenal pheochromocytoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Catecholamine levels in a 24-hour urine sample were normal in 4 patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma and increased in 36 (100%) cases with adrenal pheochromocytoma. (131)I-MIBG nuclear scan showed negative results in 4 patients (100%) with adrenal ganglioneuroma and positive results in 25 (96.2%) with adrenal pheochromocytom."
    explanation: >-
      Retrospective series of 18 adrenal ganglioneuromas and 36 adrenal
      pheochromocytomas (1999-2007; ganglioneuroma mean age 41.2 years, range
      16-67). 24-hour urinary catecholamines were raised in all 36
      pheochromocytomas and normal in the 4 ganglioneuroma patients reported,
      and 131I-MIBG was positive in 25 (96.2%) pheochromocytomas and negative in
      all 4 ganglioneuromas scanned. The ganglioneuroma numbers are small: 9
      asymptomatic ganglioneuromas under 4 cm had neither test, and the abstract
      does not say how many had urinary catecholamines measured. The result
      concerns adolescent and adult adrenal tumors. 'pheochromocytom' is
      PubMed's own spelling.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT

      (24.5%). This qualifies the adult adrenal series above, in which all 4

  - reference: PMID:24033606
    reference_title: "Adrenal ganglioneuroma: features and outcomes of 27 cases at a referral cancer centre."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Isolated AGNs do not produce catecholamines and have CT imaging characteristics that can help in distinguishing them from other adrenal and para-adrenal neoplasms."
    explanation: >-
      Conclusion of a retrospective series of 27 adrenal ganglioneuromas at a
      tertiary referral cancer center (median age 31 years, range 1.7-64), in
      which catecholamine levels were normal in all 21 patients with results.
      The authors generalize that isolated adrenal ganglioneuromas do not
      produce catecholamines; 'isolated' most likely means without a
      pheochromocytoma component, since a later systematic review lists this
      series with 11% composite tumors. The basis is 21 tested patients, and a
      catecholamine-secreting adrenal ganglioneuroma has been reported
      (PMID:35800596), so this reads as rarely rather than never. The second
      half of the sentence, on CT features, is not used here.
  - reference: PMID:35800596
    reference_title: "Rare Case of Large Catecholamine Secreting Ganglioneuroma in an Asymptomatic Elderly Male."

      Case report of an elderly man with an incidental adrenal ganglioneuroma

      the ganglioneuroma as the source of the catecholamine excess. It supports
      the statement that a catecholamine-secreting adrenal ganglioneuroma has
      been reported. It is a single case, and catecholamine secretion does not
      exclude an unsampled pheochromocytoma component.
  - reference: PMID:33721367
    reference_title: "Presentation and outcomes of adrenal ganglioneuromas: A cohort study and a systematic review of literature."
    supports: SUPPORT

    explanation: >-
      Mayo Clinic single-center cohort of 45 histologically confirmed adrenal
      ganglioneuromas (1995-2019; median age 44, range 6-87), 20 of them
      composite ganglioneuroma-pheochromocytomas. Every tumor with an
      adrenergic or noradrenergic catecholamine profile was composite, and all
      17 tested pure ganglioneuromas were non-functional. The finding holds only

      not completely exclude a pheochromocytoma component.
  - reference: PMID:33721367
    reference_title: "Presentation and outcomes of adrenal ganglioneuromas: A cohort study and a systematic review of literature."

    explanation: >-
      In the discussion of their adrenal ganglioneuroma cohort and systematic
      review, the authors state that the perioperative morbidity of an
      unrecognized pheochromocytoma makes distinguishing it from adrenal
      ganglioneuroma crucial before adrenalectomy, and that because pure and
      composite tumors overlap on imaging, any indeterminate adrenal mass should
      be tested for catecholamine excess. The first sentence frames the clinical
      reason and the second is the authors' recommendation from their imaging
      comparison; neither reports a measured rate.
- name: Schwannoma
  disease_term:
    preferred_term: schwannoma
    term:
      id: MONDO:0002546
      label: schwannoma
  description: >-
    In a pooled series of adrenal cases, most of them drawn from published
    reports, adrenal schwannoma and adrenal ganglioneuroma were similar except
    that ganglioneuroma patients were younger; all of the schwannomas and 92.5%
    of the ganglioneuromas were nonsecretory, so hormone testing does not
    separate them.
  distinguishing_features:
  - "Patients with adrenal ganglioneuroma were younger than those with adrenal schwannoma (28 vs 49.5 years) in a pooled adrenal series"
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:41563553
    reference_title: "Adrenal schwannomas and adrenal ganglioneuromas: our experience and CT characteristic-focused systematic review."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: REVIEW_SYNTHESIS
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "AS and AG were similar, except AG patients were younger (28 vs. 49.5 years, p = 0.003)."
    explanation: >-
      This pooled series combines the authors' own adrenal schwannomas (AS) and
      adrenal ganglioneuromas (AG) with histopathologically confirmed cases from
      a systematic literature review; most of the cases come from published
      reports (25 of the 30 schwannomas and 25 of the 27 ganglioneuromas), so
      the comparison is graded as a review synthesis. The authors report the two tumors as similar except for age:
      patients with ganglioneuroma were younger (28 vs 49.5 years). The
      abstract does not list which features entered this comparison, or say
      whether the ages are medians or means, and it reports separately that
      progressive delayed enhancement occurred in all AS but not in all AG, so
      'similar' should not be read as covering CT enhancement. The finding
      covers the adrenal site only.

    reference_title: "Adrenal schwannomas and adrenal ganglioneuromas: our experience and CT characteristic-focused systematic review."

      In the same pooled adrenal series, all adrenal schwannomas (AS) and 92.5%
      of adrenal ganglioneuromas (AG) were nonsecretory, so a nonsecretory mass
      does not separate the two tumors. The figures describe this selected,

treatments:
- name: Surgical Resection
  description: >-
    In German trials of localized tumors in patients aged 21 years or younger,
    no ganglioneuroma or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma progressed after
    complete resection or with residuals under 2 cm, and incomplete resection
    leaving minor residuals appeared to be sufficient treatment; the 11
    patients given chemotherapy, 2 of them with ganglioneuroma, showed no
    substantial effect, and the authors' conclusion that chemotherapy is not
    effective pools the two tumor types. Postoperative complications are
    common: short-term complications followed 43.1% of operations, most often
    neurological deficits (22.2%), in a 101-patient single-institution series;
    complications followed surgery in 11 of 51 patients (21.6%) with a resected
    retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma at a sarcoma center, one of whom died on
    postoperative day 8 of bowel ischemia; post-operative complications
    occurred in 15 of 50 surgical patients (30%) in a pediatric series, and a
    UK nationwide series reported a 19% complication rate, both pooling
    ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma. In two children with a
    VIP-secreting ganglioneuroma, VIP returned to normal after the operation
    and the diarrhea stopped.
  action_category: THERAPEUTIC
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: surgical resection
    term:
      id: NCIT:C164212
      label: Tumor Resection
  therapeutic_modality: SURGERY
  target_mechanisms:
  - target: Schwannian Stromal Development
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: >-
      Resection removes the stroma-dominant tumor tissue. In German trials of
      localized tumors, no tumor progressed after complete resection or with
      residuals under 2 cm; that this reflects removal of the stromal tissue is
      an inference, and the authors could not rule out that progression in
      large residuals came from immature components.
    evidence:
    - reference: PMID:27465021
      reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
      supports: SUPPORT
      directness: INDIRECT
      quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
      evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
      snippet: "No progression was seen after complete resection or in patients with minor tumor residuals (<2 cm), neither in patients with GN nor in patients with GNBI."
      explanation: >-
        In this retrospective study of localized ganglioneuroma (GN) and
        intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (GNBI) in children and young people
        registered in the German neuroblastoma trials NB97 and NB2004, no tumor
        was seen to progress after complete resection or when the residual left
        behind was under 2 cm on MRI. This held for GN and GNBI taken
        separately, and every progression in the series arose in a larger
        residual. The sentence reports a clinical outcome and does not mention
        the stroma. Reading it as support for this link rests on resection
        removing the stroma-dominant tumor tissue, so it is indirect. The
        authors could not rule out that progression in large residuals came
        from immature components, and they cite a suggestion that minor
        increases in tumor volume may come from Schwannian stroma
        proliferation, so the quote does not show that stromal growth is what
        resection controls.
  - target: Tumoral Vasoactive Intestinal Peptide Secretion
    treatment_effect: INHIBITS
    description: >-
      Removing a VIP-secreting tumor ended the VIP excess in two children:
      after the operation VIP was normal, falling in one child from 314 pmol/l
      before surgery to 14 pmol/l 6 months after it, and the diarrhea stopped.

- name: Watchful Observation
  description: >-
    Observation of selected asymptomatic tumors. In a single-institution series
    of 101 patients, 31 (mean age 32.3 years) were managed by observation,
    chosen mainly because the tumor lay close to major vascular or neural
    structures (74.2%); none developed new symptoms over a mean follow-up of
    90.9 months, reported as 90.9 ± 151 months, and two tumors grew minimally
    (0.08-0.17 cm/year). The authors concluded that observation is safe for
    asymptomatic tumors, particularly when adjacent to critical structures; the
    observed group was selected rather than randomized. A UK nationwide series
    of ganglioneuroma and intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma concluded that
    watchful active observation can be a safe approach, with resection reserved
    for symptomatic patients, although a small proportion of observed patients
    had their diagnosis revised to malignant at surgery, and a Hospital for Sick
    Children series concluded that, because these tumors grow slowly and
    resection can carry significant morbidity, watch-and-wait approaches should
    be considered for some patients. Both series pool ganglioneuroma with
    intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, and in both a part of the observed group
    grew or went to surgery: in the UK series 21 of 58 observed patients (36%)
    required subsequent surgery, with progressive tumor growth given as a
    reason, and in the Hospital for Sick Children series 6 of 21 observed
    patients had progressive disease and 4 later underwent resection. The
    authors of a series of 51 retroperitoneal ganglioneuromas, all of them
    resected, regard active surveillance as a reasonable option for selected
    patients with small, asymptomatic tumors, particularly those with elevated
    surgical risk.
  action_category: MONITORING
  treatment_term:
    preferred_term: watchful active observation
    term:
      id: NCIT:C25598
      label: Observation Activity
  context: Asymptomatic ganglioneuroma (the UK and Hospital for Sick Children evidence pools ganglioneuroma with intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma)
  notes: >-
    NCIT:C15722 Patient Observation fits the action more closely, but its
    parents, Surveillance (NCIT:C15719) and Healthcare Activity (NCIT:C16205),
    are not under NCIT:C25218, so the treatment-action enum rejects it.
    NCIT:C25598 Observation Activity reaches NCIT:C25218 through Diagnostic
    Procedure (NCIT:C18020) and is bound here for the observation step of a
    monitoring strategy (action_category MONITORING), as in the
    Ganglioneuroblastoma entry.
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:42537227
    reference_title: "A comprehensive analysis of ganglioneuromas: a 30-year experience of 101 patients."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT

No patient in the observation group developed new symptoms, and 2 tumors exhibited minimal interval growth (0.08-0.17 cm/year)."
    explanation: >-
      In this single-institution retrospective series, 31 patients (mean age
      32.3 years) were managed with observation and followed for a mean of 90.9
      months. The abstract reports this follow-up as 90.9 ± 151 months without
      saying what the ± value is; a spread larger than the mean suggests that
      follow-up length varied widely between patients. Proximity to major
      vascular or neural structures was the main reason for choosing
      observation (74.2%), which is a reason for the choice, not a comparison
      between tumors in different locations. No observed patient developed new
      symptoms, and two tumors grew minimally, at 0.08-0.17 cm/year. The
      abstract gives no imaging schedule, does not state whether the observed
      patients had symptoms when observation began, and does not say how their
      diagnosis was established.
  - reference: PMID:42537227
    reference_title: "A comprehensive analysis of ganglioneuromas: a 30-year experience of 101 patients."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "Observation is safe for asymptomatic tumors, particularly when adjacent to critical structures, with no growth observed on long-term follow-up, except for minimal growth in few cases."
    explanation: >-
      This single-institution retrospective review covers 101 patients
      diagnosed with ganglioneuroma between 1995 and 2025. The authors conclude

      than randomized. The series did not compare observed tumors next to

      recommendation, not a finding that observation is safer there. The
      abstract does not define safety; the outcomes it reports for the observed
      group are no new symptoms and at most minimal tumor growth.
  - reference: PMID:39582110

    explanation: >-
      This single-center retrospective cohort (Hospital for Sick Children,
      1990-2014) covers 67 children and adolescents with ganglioneuroma (50) or
      intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma (17), 21 of them observed. Its conclusion
      recommends considering watch-and-wait for some patients, citing slow tumor
      growth and the significant morbidity resection can carry. It supports this
      rationale and recommendation only; 6 of the 21 observed patients had
      progressive disease, and the abstract does not report outcomes for
      ganglioneuroma separately.

    reference_title: "Management of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma Intermixed: A United Kingdom Children's Cancer and Leukaemia Group (UK CCLG) Nationwide Study Report."

    snippet: "In all 201 (78%) had initial surgery and 58 (22%) were observed. Overall survival was 98%. Twenty-one of 58 observed (36%) required subsequent surgery due to progressive tumour growth (52%)."
    explanation: >-
      In this UK CCLG series of 259 patients with non-metastatic ganglioneuroma
      or intermixed ganglioneuroblastoma, 58 (22%) were observed initially, and
      21 of them (36%) later required surgery, with progressive tumor growth
      given as a reason; the abstract does not say what the 52% beside it is a
      share of. Overall survival in the whole cohort was 98%. This qualifies
      the safety conclusion rather than refuting it: observation was followed
      by surgery in 36% of observed patients, and the figures pool the two
      tumor types and do not report ganglioneuroma separately. The support
      for observation is indirect, because the sentence reports what happened
      to observed patients, not that observation was safe.

      The conclusion of this retrospective series of 51 resected primary
      retroperitoneal ganglioneuromas states that active surveillance is a
      reasonable option for selected patients with small, asymptomatic tumors,
      particularly those with elevated surgical risk. Every patient in the
      series was resected, as its inclusion criteria required, so the
      recommendation is the authors' judgement, which their Discussion
      supports with other published series, not an outcome of observed
      patients in this study.
discussions:
- discussion_id: gn_origin
  prompt: >-
    Does every ganglioneuroma arise by maturation of a neuroblastoma or
    ganglioneuroblastoma, or can some arise without passing through a
    neuroblastoma or ganglioneuroblastoma stage?
  kind: CONTROVERSY
  status: OPEN
  attaches_to:
  - pathophysiology#Neuroblast-to-Ganglion Cell Maturation
  - pathophysiology#AKT-mTOR-S6 Pathway Activation
  rationale: >-
    Evidence items use this convention: SUPPORT means ganglioneuroma arises by
    maturation of a neuroblastic tumor; REFUTE means at least some
    ganglioneuromas arise without passing through a neuroblastoma or
    ganglioneuroblastoma stage, against the proposal that all do. Both
    readings start from sympathetic neuroblasts, which the authors of the
    zebrafish study name as the common origin of ganglioneuroma and
    neuroblastoma; the question is whether a neuroblastic tumor comes first.
    The authors of the German NB97/NB2004 series note
    that a good part of ganglioneuroma has been hypothesized to originate from
    neuroblastoma, but that whether this holds for all ganglioneuromas, as
    Shimada proposed, remains unproven, and they read the rise of median age
    with the grade of differentiation as indirect support. A 2026 pathology
    review recounts that Cushing and Wolbach, comparing one patient's specimen
    at diagnosis with one resected 10 years later, reported that a malignant
    neuroblastoma could differentiate into a benign ganglioneuroma. In a 2022
    case report, ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma at different sites of
    one patient shared the same molecular signatures, which the authors read
    as probable spontaneous differentiation from neuroblastoma to
    ganglioneuroma. In zebrafish, constitutively activated Akt2 in the
    sympathetic nervous system produced low-grade ganglioneuroma, and the fish
    did not develop neuroblastoma; this is a model of ganglioneuroma forming
    without a neuroblastoma stage, and its tumors had poor Schwannian stroma.
    The authors regard the model as faithful to human ganglioneuroma, which
    also shows AKT activation, but whether human ganglioneuromas form without a
    neuroblastoma stage has not been shown. The answer decides whether
    ganglioneuroma biology can be read as the end state of favorable
    neuroblastoma.
  posed_date: '2026-10-28T00:00:00Z'
  evidence:
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: BACKGROUND
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "It has been hypothesized that a good part of GN originates from neuroblastoma. ... However, whether this is true for all GN, as proposed by Shimada ... still remains unproven."
    explanation: >-
      In the discussion of this retrospective study of localized neuroblastic
      tumors registered in the German neuroblastoma trials, the authors restate
      the hypothesis, which they trace to Cushing and to later reports in stage
      4S and localized neuroblastoma, that a good part of ganglioneuroma arises
      from neuroblastoma, and add that whether this is true for all
      ganglioneuromas, as Shimada proposed, remains unproven. The quoted
      sentences summarize earlier reports rather than the study's own data. The
      first sentence reports a hypothesis rather than asserting it, so it
      supports maturation from a neuroblastic tumor only at one remove, through
      the earlier reports the authors summarize (Cushing; stage 4S and localized
      neuroblastoma); the second records that the claim for all ganglioneuromas
      is unproven.
  - reference: PMID:27465021
    reference_title: "Treatment and outcome of Ganglioneuroma and Ganglioneuroblastoma intermixed."
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "An indirect support for this hypothesis can be seen by our observation that patients with GN and GNBI are older at diagnosis than those with immature NT. As Fig. 1 shows, the grade of neuroblastic differentiation increases with the median age at diagnosis. This is in line with the theory that mature NT might just have had enough time to mature in situ before they were discovered."
    explanation: >-
      The same authors take their own age gradient as indirect support for
      maturation: patients with ganglioneuroma or intermixed
      ganglioneuroblastoma, taken together, were older at diagnosis than those
      with immature tumors, which fits mature tumors having had time to mature
      in situ. The gradient compares different patients at diagnosis, and the
      paper reports no tumor seen to mature during follow-up; the authors add in
      the next paragraph that ultrasound check-ups of infants and young
      toddlers could find small immature neuroblastomas early, which bears on
      the same age difference without involving maturation.
  - reference: PMID:41612478

    explanation: >-
      In its history of neuroblastic-tumor pathology, this 2026 review recounts
      that Cushing and Wolbach compared one patient's tumor at diagnosis with
      the specimen resected 10 years later and reported that a malignant
      neuroblastoma could differentiate into a benign ganglioneuroma. It is the
      review's account of a single human case it did not study, hence
      HUMAN_CLINICAL with REVIEW_SYNTHESIS; this review's classification-rule
      sentences, which report no patient data, are graded OTHER elsewhere in the
      KB. On the review's account a neuroblastoma matured into a ganglioneuroma
      in at least one patient. The sentence does not say whether this was
      spontaneous or followed treatment, and it says nothing about where
      ganglioneuromas come from in general.
  - reference: PMID:34054050
    reference_title: "A Metastatic Neuroblastic Tumor in a 28-Month-old Boy: Unusual Spontaneous Regression From Neuroblastoma to Ganglioneuroma?"
    supports: SUPPORT
    directness: DIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "A pathologic examination of tumors from different sites revealed both GNB and ganglioneuroma. A genetic comparison between these tumors identified the same molecular signatures, suggesting the possibility of spontaneous differentiation in the remaining GNB. ... Even if unusual, we have to recognize probable spontaneous differentiation from neuroblastoma to GNB and then to ganglioneuroma, even in sites of bone metastasis."
    explanation: >-
      Single case report (abstract only). A 28-month-old boy had adrenal
      ganglioneuroblastoma with metastases to subcutaneous nodules, a lymph node
      and multiple bones. Tumors from different sites showed
      ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma and shared the same molecular
      signatures. The authors conclude that the tumor probably differentiated
      spontaneously from neuroblastoma to ganglioneuroblastoma and then to
      ganglioneuroma, even in bone metastases, and they hedge this ('probable';
      the title ends in a question mark). The abstract reports no neuroblastoma
      histology and no serial biopsies, so the neuroblastoma starting point and
      the sequence are inferred from histology at different sites, and
      'spontaneous' rests on the patient staying healthy without aggressive
      chemotherapy. For one patient, the item supports ganglioneuroma arising
      by maturation of a neuroblastic tumor; it says nothing about how often
      this happens.
  - reference: PMID:32728700
    reference_title: "Ganglioneuromas are driven by activated AKT and can be therapeutically targeted with mTOR inhibitors."
    supports: REFUTE
    directness: INDIRECT
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: MODEL_ORGANISM
    snippet: "Thus, AKT activation caused a modest increase in cell proliferation of ganglioneuroma, which although much less than that induced by MYCN in neuroblastoma nevertheless resulted in sympathoadrenal lineage cell hyperplasia, as reflected by the appearance of low-grade ganglioneuroma in transgenic zebrafish. ... These fish did not develop neuroblastoma, despite the fact that the same promoter-driven other oncogenes such as MYCN or c-MYC induces high-grade neuroblastoma in this model (Tao et al., 2017; Zimmerman et al., 2018; Zhu et al., 2012)."
    explanation: >-
      In transgenic zebrafish expressing constitutively activated myr-Akt2 in
      the sympathetic nervous system, AKT activation caused a modest increase
      in proliferation and sympathoadrenal lineage cell hyperplasia, which the
      authors relate to the appearance of low-grade ganglioneuroma, and the
      discussion states that these fish did not develop neuroblastoma, although
      the same promoter driving MYCN or c-MYC induces neuroblastoma in this
      model. In this model, then, a ganglioneuroma formed with no neuroblastoma
      found, which argues against the proposal that every ganglioneuroma is a
      matured neuroblastoma, not against some arising that way. The authors do
      not ask whether human ganglioneuromas pass through a neuroblastoma stage:
      they state that ganglioneuroma and neuroblastoma both originate from
      migratory sympathetic neuroblasts of one progenitor lineage, and they
      propose that somatically acquired mutations or epigenetic alterations in
      the PI3K-AKT-mTOR pathway may initiate ganglioneuroma, so reading the model
      against the all-from-neuroblastoma proposal is the curator's inference.
      The authors regard the model as faithful to human ganglioneuroma, which
      also shows AKT activation, but the fish tumors had poor Schwannian stroma,
      whereas human ganglioneuroma is defined by dominant Schwannian stroma, and
      the model does not show that human ganglioneuromas form without a
      neuroblastoma stage.
  - reference: PMID:41858373
    reference_title: "Telomere maintenance mechanisms are activated in ganglioneuroblastoma and ganglioneuroma."
    supports: NO_EVIDENCE
    quote_role: PRIMARY_RESULT
    evidence_source: HUMAN_CLINICAL
    snippet: "ALT was identified in 38 NB cases (38/255, 15%) and 6 GNB cases (6/48, 12.5%), but not in GN. ... In the present study, high expression of TERT mRNA and ALT as TMM in NB was observed more frequently in the older age group (>18 months), INSS stage 4, and the HR group, with statistical significance, as in previous reports (15,16)."
    explanation: >-
      In this Japanese series of neuroblastic tumors sampled at diagnosis,
      alternative lengthening of telomeres (ALT) was found in 15% of
      neuroblastomas and in no ganglioneuroma, and a telomere maintenance
      mechanism (TMM: ALT and/or high TERT expression) in 1 of 18
      ganglioneuromas. Graded NO_EVIDENCE because neither result says whether a
      neuroblastic tumor came first. In neuroblastoma both mechanisms were
      found more often in patients older than 18 months, at INSS stage 4 and in
      the high-risk group, while the maturation proposal concerns biologically
      favorable tumors (PMID:41612478), so little or no telomere maintenance in
      ganglioneuroma is what either answer to the question predicts. The
      authors draw no conclusion about the origin of ganglioneuroma, they leave
      the clinical significance of telomere maintenance in ganglioneuroma to
      longer follow-up, and the paper's ganglioneuroma denominators differ
      between its abstract, Results and Discussion.

Create: Ganglioneuroma · 2026-10-28T21:25:06Z · View source

New entry for ganglioneuroma (MONDO:0005033), the fully differentiated, benign member of the peripheral neuroblastic tumours. Part of the neuroblastoma expansion series (issue 15822); claim issue 15823. Content: two INPC subtypes (maturing, NCIT:C42064; mature, NCIT:C42065); four pathophysiology nodes (neuroblast-to-ganglion cell maturation, Schwannian stromal development, tumoral vasoactive intestinal peptide secretion, and AKT-mTOR-S6 pathway activation from human immunohistochemistry, left without causal edges as its notes explain); three histopathology rows; eight phenotypes (retroperitoneal, posterior mediastinal and pelvic masses, pain, watery diarrhoea and hypokalaemia from VIP secretion, raised urinary HVA and VMA); a MYCN amplification biomarker row (2 of 34 GN-maturing tumours amplified in the CCG/COG series, 0 of 9 in a second series, with no frequency band because the two small series disagree); four differentials (ganglioneuroblastoma, neuroblastoma, adrenal gland pheochromocytoma, schwannoma); surgical resection and watchful observation; and a CONTROVERSY discussion on whether ganglioneuroma arises by maturation of a neuroblastic tumour. 134 evidence items from 25 references, 17 of them newly cached. The pheochromocytoma differential is bound to MONDO:0004974 (adrenal gland pheochromocytoma) because all of its evidence concerns adrenal tumours; MONDO does not file that term under MONDO:0035540, the term of the Pheochromocytoma_Paraganglioma entry, so the row gets no internal page link. Source report: research/Ganglioneuroma-deep-research-claude_code.md (claude_code provider), re-validated before commit, 82 of 82 references resolved. just preflight-dr returns WARN; both flagged passages are parsing artefacts (line 742 reads "PTEN/BMPR1A hgnc:1076", where hgnc:1076 is BMPR1A, and line 1332 writes each identifier before its symbol), and MONDO records no causal gene for this term. The entry binds no gene identifier from the report; hgnc:7559 (MYCN) was read from cache/hgnc/terms.csv. Claude Code ▸
1. Disease Information
claude-haiku-4-5-20251001, claude-opus-5 92 citations 2026-10-28T16:38:09.357909

1. Disease Information

1.1 Overview

Ganglioneuroma is a rare, benign, well-differentiated tumour of neural-crest origin arising from the sympathetic nervous system, composed of mature ganglion cells embedded in a dominant Schwannian (Schwann-cell + collagen) stroma. It arises anywhere along the sympathetic chain from skull base to pelvis, most often retroperitoneum, posterior mediastinum, adrenal gland and pelvis.

Orphanet's definition, verbatim:

"Ganglioneuroma is a rare tumor of neuroepithelial tissue, a benign and well-differentiated tumor of neural crest origin" — Orphanet, ORPHA:251992

Orphanet continues that it "originates from the sympathetic nervous system and comprises ganglion cells and stromal Schwann cells," with common sites "adrenal glands, retroperitoneum, posterior mediastinum, and pelvis," and records associations with "neurofibromatosis type I, multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2B, and Turner syndrome."

1.2 Position in the INPC

The International Neuroblastoma Pathology Classification (INPC, the "Shimada system") defines four pNT categories (Shimada et al., Cancer 1999;86:349–363, PMID:10421272; and Cancer 1999;86:364–372, PMID:10421273):

  1. Neuroblastoma — Schwannian stroma-poor
  2. Ganglioneuroblastoma, intermixed — Schwannian stroma-rich (MONDO:0003326)
  3. Ganglioneuroma — Schwannian stroma-dominant ← this entry
  4. Ganglioneuroblastoma, nodular — composite (MONDO:0003325); revised into favourable/unfavourable subsets by Peuchmaur et al., Cancer 2003;98:2274–2281, PMID:14601099

GN has two histological subtypes, maturing and mature, and both are Favorable Histology regardless of patient age (Shimada H, Sano H, Hazard FK. Clin Pediatr Hematol Oncol 2020;27:73–86, doi:10.15264/cpho.2020.27.2.73). That review states GN represents approximately 3% of cases reviewed at the COG Neuroblastoma Pathology Reference Laboratory.

A 2026 update of the pathology of pNTs is available: Conces MR, Shibui Y, Shimada H. "Pathology of peripheral neuroblastic tumors." Diagnostic Pathology 2026;21:22, doi:10.1186/s13000-026-01758-y — open access (full text was paywall-redirected in this session; the abstract confirms the 1984→1999→2003 INPC lineage).

Related MONDO terms confirmed present in the local cache: MONDO:0003325 nodular ganglioneuroblastoma, MONDO:0003326 intermixed schwannian stroma-rich ganglioneuroblastoma, MONDO:0005035 ganglioneuroblastoma, MONDO:0005072 neuroblastoma, MONDO:0006076 adrenal gland neuroblastoma.

1.4 Synonyms

ganglioneuroma (Schwannian stroma-dominant); mature ganglioneuroma; maturing ganglioneuroma; benign ganglioneuroma; benign neural crest tumour. Site-qualified variants in common use: adrenal ganglioneuroma (AGN), retroperitoneal ganglioneuroma (RGN), paraspinal/dumbbell ganglioneuroma, presacral ganglioneuroma, intestinal/diffuse ganglioneuromatosis (a distinct, usually syndromic, polytopic form — see §2.2 and §6.3).

1.5 Evidence provenance

All content below is from aggregated disease-level resources and institutional/registry case series. There are no EHR-derived or patient-level datasets for GN. The three largest modern series — 259 (UK national registry), 101 (single institution, 30 years), 51 (single sarcoma centre) — are all retrospective chart reviews. No randomised trial exists, and none is plausible at this incidence.

2. Etiology

2.1 Disease causal factors

GN is a somatic neoplasm of a sympathoadrenal neural-crest derivative. There is no infectious, dietary, or occupational cause described anywhere in the literature reviewed. Two causal models compete (detailed in §6):

  • Terminal-maturation model (INPC consensus). GN is the end-state of a neuroblastic tumour that differentiated. Shimada et al. state this explicitly: ganglioneuromas represent the "final stage of tumor maturation," such that "all Ganglioneuromas were once Neuroblastomas in their early stage of tumor development" (Shimada 2020).
  • De novo AKT-driven model. Tao et al. found an active AKT–mTOR–S6 axis in human GN but not neuroblastoma, and showed that constitutive AKT in the zebrafish sympathetic nervous system produces GN without passing through a neuroblastoma state (PMID:32728700). Verbatim from the abstract:

"Zebrafish transgenic for constitutively activated myr-Akt2 in the sympathetic nervous system were found to develop ganglioneuroma without progression to neuroblastoma." "Our results implicate activated AKT as a tumorigenic driver in ganglioneuroma."

These are not reconcilable as stated, and the field has not resolved them. Note what each predicts about the same patient: the maturation model predicts GN should carry residual neuroblastoma-type segmental aberrations; the de novo model predicts it should not. The telomere data in §4.2 are the best available discriminating evidence and favour a genomically distinct entity.

2.2 Genetic risk factors

Germline syndromes. GN is not itself inherited, but three autosomal-dominant syndromes carry a materially raised risk, and in each the ganglioneuromatous lesion is frequently the first manifestation:

  • MEN2B (RET hgnc:9967). A single germline mutation at codon 918 (M918T, exon 16) accounts for ~95% of MEN2B. Diffuse intestinal ganglioneuromatosis is a universal extra-endocrine feature and typically precedes medullary thyroid carcinoma by years. Gastrointestinal onset occurred in 29/55 (52.3%) of reported cases before age 1, and constipation was the commonest symptom (72.7%) (Intestinal ganglioneuromatosis as an early extra-endocrine manifestation of MEN2B, PMC10848182; Timely diagnosis of MEN2B by identification of intestinal ganglioneuromatosis, Springer; role of rectal biopsy, PMID:27899172). Germline codon-918 mutation has also been found in apparently isolated intestinal ganglioneuromatosis (J Clin Endocrinol Metab 1998;83:4191).
  • PTEN hamartoma tumour syndrome / Cowden syndrome (PTEN hgnc:9588). Ganglioneuromatous polyposis, diffuse gastric ganglioneuromatosis, and uveal ganglioneuroma are all reported presentations (PMC5889855; PMC4862217; PMID:23512313; uveal ganglioneuroma, PMC5566766).
  • Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1 hgnc:7765). GN in NF1 is real but uncommon. In an Italian multicentre registry study, 8 children with neuroblastic tumours (0.2% of registered patients) met NF1 criteria; of those, 5 were neuroblastoma, 1 ganglioneuroblastoma, 2 ganglioneuroma (PMC9673013). Co-occurrence with pheochromocytoma is described (PMID:17954422), and bladder and cervical GN have been reported in NF1 (PMC9776796; PMC13492013).
  • Turner syndrome — recorded by Orphanet as an association. [No primary citation located in this session; treat as Orphanet-level only.]

PHOX2B hgnc:9143 — one reported instance, not a susceptibility claim. In a familial neuroblastic-tumour report, a PHOX2B variant inherited from the father was followed by that father developing a well-differentiated ganglioneuroma at age 40 — the single clearest human observation that a germline neurocristopathy allele can yield a differentiated rather than malignant tumour (Identifying a novel PHOX2B gene variant in a neuroblastoma family, ScienceDirect; see also Analysis of sporadic neuroblastic tumors reveals a novel PHOX2B mutation). n=1; do not curate as a susceptibility locus.

Susceptibility loci / GWAS. No GWAS, no polygenic risk score, and no common-variant association for GN. The neuroblastoma GWAS literature does not stratify by INPC category.

2.3 Environmental risk factors

  • Prior therapeutic irradiation is the one documented exogenous risk factor, and it acts on outcome rather than occurrence: MPNST has arisen inside a benign ganglioneuroma 12 years after radiotherapy for a childhood neuroblastic tumour (PMID:10755757), and after radiotherapy for a cerebellopontine-angle ganglioneuroma (PMID:42120254).
  • Age is the strongest demographic correlate (see §9).
  • No toxin, pollutant, occupational exposure, dietary factor, smoking, or alcohol association is reported.

2.4 Protective factors

None documented, genetic or environmental. The absence is informative rather than a gap in searching: GN has no modifiable risk factor to protect against, and no gnomAD-level protective allele has been sought. Plausibly the closest thing to a "protective factor" in the pNT family is the differentiation-promoting Schwann-cell stroma itself (§6.2) — but that is a mechanism within the tumour, not a population-level exposure, and should not be curated as PROTECTS_AGAINST.

2.5 Gene–environment interactions

Only one interaction is evidenced, and it is iatrogenic: germline NF1 loss + therapeutic irradiation → MPNST arising within a ganglioneuroma. The 2025 rectovaginal-septum case is the worked example — a 29-year-old woman with NF1 whose lesion was called ganglioneuroma on biopsy and proved MPNST on resection (PMC12221925). No CTD/PheGenI GxE entry exists for GN.

3. Phenotypes

3.1 The dominant phenotype is absence of phenotype

Across all three modern series, most GN are incidental:

Series n Incidental
Texakalidis 2026, J Neurosurg (PMID:42537227) 101 72%
Li 2025, Front Surg (PMID:41306794) 51 56.9%
Burnand 2025, UK CCLG (PMID:39582110) 259 — (symptom data by site)

Verbatim: "Most lesions (72%) were incidental findings." (PMID:42537227)

This matters for the KB entry: the modal patient has no phenotype, and symptomatic phenotypes are almost all mass effect, not tumour biology.

3.2 Phenotype table with HPO bindings

All HP CURIEs below were resolved in cache/hp/terms.csv this session except where flagged.
